#3056. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#3050. Walker Kessler is having more of an impact than it looks

Since the Lakers acquired Walker Kessler over the summer, most of the conversation regarding his play, and subsequent impact, has been tied to the cost paid to land him.

The multiple years and millions of dollars, the draft picks and swaps, each have served as footnotes after every game thus far.

While fair given what the Lakers need from him going forward, it has also put increased emphasis on his raw production rather than influence. If you're into that kind of thing, Kessler's per-game numbers are solid. His 14.4 points, 10.2 rebounds and four stocks nightly will play. However, it doesn't capture the full spectrum of his impact.

On offense, Kessler is still navigating life as Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves' screener. It's an entirely new world compared to the barren guard groups he played next to with the Utah Jazz.

Things like timing, pick angles and court-mapping will take time. But the early results have been encouraging nevertheless.

After his dunk-centric eruption on opening night, defenses have already begun selling out in the paint to take away his lob and cut chances.

Due to this, Kessler has not been able to duplicate his scoring outbursts, but it hasn't lessened his value to the offense either. A direct result of teams loading up and tagging Kessler — often with multiple defenders — is that it has created open shots from the perimeter, as seen in the clips below.

Kessler's roll gravity also extends to his rebounding ability. As one of the league's best second-chance creators, and simply being a massive person, Kessler often draws multiple players to him whenever a shot goes up.

This fear is well warranted. The Lakers' offensive rebound percentage jolts up nearly 10% when Kessler is on the floor compared to off. Like a black hole, this sucks up defenders from the corners and creates quality looks for shooters. The Lakers are currently shooting 43.9% from the corners this season, the second-best mark in the league.

Beyond the attention Kessler attracts on offense simply from existing, his omnipresence is probably best demonstrated with the impact he's had on the Lakers' defense.

As the backbone and backline, Kessler's presence in the paint has allowed the team's wings to be extremely aggressive shocking the ball with the trust that the center will clean up any mistakes. This disruptive style has resulted in the Lakers being one of the best defenses in terms of forced turnovers.

And when his teammates do get burned for gambling or getting blown by, Kessler has been iron-clad protecting the basket.

Not only are opposing teams shooting just 26.9% of their shots at the rim when Kessler is on the floor — would rank fourth-best in the NBA — they are also missing once he meets them there.

Among the 12 players who have defended at least 50 shots within six feet this season, Kessler ranks first in defended shooting percentage against (45.1%) while also contesting the second-most attempts behind only Victor Wembanyama, according to the league's tracking data.

Kessler's deterrence and effectiveness in shutting off the paint is not solely due to his size.

His height and frightening wingspan do help, yes. But what drives his success is his impressive motor. With every second jump contest, race down the floor and closeout to a 3-point shooter, Kessler's care factor is never in doubt. That goes a long way to actualizing his defensive upside.

Despite how hard he ultimately tries, the Lakers are learning firsthand that Kessler can't be everywhere at once. This is never more true than the defensive drop-off once he's on the bench.

Through their first nine games, the Lakers have allowed a defensive rating of 110.1 with Kessler on the court compared to 117.2 when he's been off. That's a discrepancy that is the equivalent of the team performing like the eighth-best defense versus the 26th.

While his individual numbers will likely fluctuate nightly, and even quarterly, as illustrated by his dominant final frame — 10 points, five rebounds, four blocks and a steal — against the Sacramento Kings on Wednesday, Kessler has proven to be more than suited for the role bestowed upon him.

He may not be the thundering force that Dončić is or as crafty as Reaves, but Kessler's job as the Lakers' silent protector has been as critical to the team's strong start.

It won't always look like he's had a good game in the box score. But just watch how that opposing ball-handler goes wide-eyed and makes a U-turn when the giant in purple and gold makes his stand.

More than any numbers can ever quantify, Kessler's game is measured in fear. And the Lakers are benefiting from it so far.

All stats courtesy of Cleaning the Glass unless otherwise stated. You can follow Alex on Bluesky at @alexregla.bsky.social

Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#3047. 6-2 Jaguars Facing Difficult Brian Thomas Balancing Act

With the NFL trade deadline arriving Tuesday, few Jacksonville Jaguars storylines carry as much league-wide attention as the future of wide receiver Brian Thomas Jr. His diminished role since Week 4 has fueled a steady stream of speculation about what it means for his long-term place in Jacksonville, and the answer is slightly more complicated than simple usage charts suggest.

In a recent report, NFL Network's Cam Wolfe added some important context to the recent BTJ buzz. According to Wolfe, Thomas and Travis Hunter are close friends. Thomas is also well liked in the Jaguars' locker room, and his speed remains an asset even without the production to match. Wolfe also reported that the Jaguars have wondered whether last year's Tyson Campbell trade hurt their immediate locker room energy, and that the organization has "learned from it."

That last point deserves attention. After the Campbell trade to Cleveland in Week 6 of 2025, some players were reportedly unhappy with the move, and Jacksonville followed it with back-to-back tough losses to the Seattle Seahawks and Los Angeles Rams. As is often the case in the NFL, winning will always cure the discomfort, however. The Jaguars closed out 2025 by going 9-1, and the locker room appeared no worse for wear.

Still, it's easy to see how that experience could shape the team's approach to dealing with the third-year receiver this time around. However, it's hard to argue that the team made the wrong 2025 decision, based on the on-field outcomes, and their defensive scheme not matching the player. That's the key piece that makes this such a fascinating conversation. Where is the line between doing whatever is necessary to win versus doing what is needed for the locker room to be together?

The numbers, on the surface, show a player whose role has shrunk considerably. Thomas currently ranks fourth on the team in targets (21), fifth on the team in receptions through eight games played (10), and seventh in yards (117) behind rookie Josh Cameron who has 123. Over his last four games combined, he has just one catch, a 12-yard touchdown against the Indianapolis Colts. On Thursday, he logged only 19 snaps against the Baltimore Ravens, a game in which he was relegated to the fourth receiver spot. However, that trend didn't start in Week 8. Thomas played a career-low 15 snaps against the Houston Texans in Week 6, before the Bye Week. After that game, head coach Liam Coen described the team's confidence in Thomas as a week-to-week matter and said simply, "Guys have to step up and make plays."

Ahead of the Colts game, Coen expressed faith that Thomas would continue helping the offense make plays. He quickly supported that faith, securing the opening-drive touchdown, his lone catch of the game.

Meanwhile, around the league, interest is as real as ever. ESPN's Dan Graziano reported that opposing teams are asking about Jacksonville's plans for Thomas and are expected to continue reaching out before the November 10 deadline. ESPN's Jeremy Fowler reported that several executives believe Thomas's days in Jacksonville are numbered, and noted that a change of scenery could bring out the best in him. For a former 2024 Rookie of the Year finalist who topped 1,200 receiving yards as a rookie, that kind of interest is not surprising. What would be surprising is the team trading that caliber of player away for less than a first-round pick.

Thomas is not publicly asking to be traded. By all accounts, he's a hard worker and has embraced the team-first approach being built. But within Liam Coen's offense, his role appears to be that of the sacrificial X, stretching the defense vertically to open space underneath for the middle-of-the-field throws that drive the scheme. With Trevor Lawrence at his best working inside the hashes, that job rarely comes paired with a major statistical output. Jakobi Meyers, Parker Washington, Josh Cameron, and the Jacksonville tight end group all share that strength. Thomas doesn't, and that's okay. Thomas' role can be valuable to the offense without ever showing up in his stat line, which is an extremely difficult reality for a player of his pedigree.

Take the Baltimore matchup as an example: while Thomas was only on the field for 19 total snaps, he ran a route on 11 of Trevor Lawrence's 26 pass attempts (42.3%). Meanwhile, the team built up a 20-point third quarter lead in the rainy Baltimore night. On those 11 dropbacks, Lawrence was 6/11 (54.5%) for 109 yards, with four of the team's 11 passing first downs (36.4%), and the only passing touchdown – a 50 yarder to Parker Washington. The argument that BTJ pulls the primary coverage or opens up space could be supported.

This is a delicate situation. The Jaguars have spent two years shaping a finely tuned locker room, and they're looking to mold this roster into a legitimate Super Bowl contender. Trading away a well-liked player who is also a close friend of Hunter's carries risks that don't clearly show up on a stats sheet. For that reason, I expect Jacksonville to be buyers only at Tuesday's deadline.

Coen touched on the locker room impacts of prospective trades in his Friday media availability:

"How does it affect things moving forward, cap, future, other players, how we can resign or not, other guys, there's so much that goes into this time of the year. Also, being cognizant of the group that you have in the locker room and making sure that the guys all know in the building we're trying to do everything we can as an organization to make the best decisions for the group and for our team, because those guys are so important to what we're doing. It's a fine line. It's definitely a balancing act for sure, and everything that comes across your desk, you've got to make sure that you're looking at it through that lens; at least we are."

The one exception I could see would be a trading partner offering a corresponding move that makes sense for both sides, such as a deal that returns defensive tackle or safety talent, which effectively forces the team's hand in moving Thomas. With injuries already thinning the safety room, that kind of return is the only scenario where moving Thomas feels justified for a team with the roster Jacksonville has built.

#3039. The best version of Devin Booker is the one attacking early

Sometimes all it takes is a spark, however small, to start a fire and change a team's trajectory. Even if that spark comes against an opponent you wouldn't necessarily consider quality.

The Phoenix Suns' 117-104 victory over the Orlando Magic might be that spark, although with Houston and San Antonio staring them down on the schedule over the next few days, there's a chance we won't look back at this game as the moment that ignited some great blaze. But there were things we witnessed that can be deposited into the old adjustments bank, lessons that could serve as valuable reference points when trying to manufacture future victories.

One of those lessons, as obvious as it might seem, is that feeding Devin Booker early remains one of the best ways to give this team an opportunity to win basketball games.

Booker is the $57 million man, and constructing a roster that forces him to assume duplicative responsibilities within the starting lineup isn't exactly ideal. Too often, we see Booker drift away from his natural role as a shooting guard and become the primary playmaker, tasked with creating opportunities for everyone else rather than focusing on what he does best.

Against Orlando, we saw something different. In the first half, Booker went 8-of-13 from the field, including 3-of-4 from beyond the arc and a perfect 4-of-4 from the free-throw line, scoring 23 points while establishing himself as the focal point of the offense.

Granted, this was the Orlando Magic. Quality opponents have demonstrated the ability to scheme Booker out of games, as we saw earlier this season against a shorthanded Oklahoma City Thunder team. And that's precisely why the argument for having a quality facilitator persists.

When you have somebody capable of assuming the responsibility of distributing the basketball, opposing defenses have another variable to account for. They have to dedicate attention to disrupting that player's ability to initiate the offense, which can create more opportunities for Booker to operate as a scorer. And when Booker gets going early, it creates opportunities for everyone around him. As he begins to command the defensive gravity we know he possesses, teammates find themselves with more space and cleaner looks. The hope is that by the time an opposing defense figures out how to slow him down, Booker has already inflicted enough damage to force them into scrambling, opening up opportunities for the rest of the roster to capitalize.

Getting Devin Booker engaged early should be the North Star for this team. If opposing defenses are finding ways to take the ball out of his hands, then it's the coaching staff's responsibility to scheme ways to get it back into his hands in situations where he can be effective.

Here's a fun stat for you. Throughout his career, Booker has attempted 13 or more field goals in the first half of a game 110 times. In those games, the Suns have a .582 winning percentage. And when Booker does what he did last night, shooting 60% or better from the field while attempting at least 13 shots in the first half, that winning percentage climbs to .727. What's interesting is that since the acquisition of Bradley Beal prior to the 2023-24 season, Booker has attempted 13 or more field goals in the first half 30 times. The other 80 times occurred pre-Beal, with 46 occurring alongside Chris Paul.

I'm certainly not breaking any new ground by suggesting that your most skilled scorer should be looking to score early and often, especially when doing so creates opportunities for the players around him. But that's exactly what we witnessed against Orlando. Booker scored only seven points in the second half, yet he added four assists as Dillon Brooks and Miles Bridges began finding more opportunities to contribute offensively, benefiting from the defensive gravity Booker had established earlier in the game.

Perhaps this needs to become a point of emphasis for the coaching staff, along with a mental adjustment for Booker himself. Because when you look at the rosters he's played alongside since Chris Paul left town, it's difficult to argue that they've consistently been constructed to maximize his greatest strength, which can create a malaise and affinity for falling into distribution responsibilities rather than getting yours.

Playing alongside Bradley Beal, whose availability was frequently compromised by injuries, eventually led to stretches in which Eric Gordon or Tyus Jones ran the offense. Now Booker finds himself sharing the backcourt with Jalen Green, another player whose natural instincts lean toward scoring rather than facilitating. None of those combinations has been particularly conducive to allowing Booker to operate consistently in the role where he's most dangerous.

While there are legitimate criticisms of Booker's game, particularly his struggles executing in clutch situations and the difficulties he encounters when more physical defenders impose their will on him, those concerns shouldn't prevent us from recognizing what he does well. When Booker establishes himself offensively early, forces defenses to react, and creates opportunities for his teammates to capitalize, that's the version of Devin Booker this team needs. And when that approach contributes to a much-needed victory, it deserves recognition and applause.

The Suns aren't going to solve every roster deficiency overnight, and the challenges of getting Booker quality looks against better competition aren't going anywhere. But there's something to be said for simplifying the approach and putting your best player in positions to succeed. Orlando provided a reminder of what happens when Booker dictates the offense rather than reacting to it. If Phoenix is searching for a spark that can become something sustainable, getting Booker going early seems like a pretty damn good place to start.

#3037. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

An official website of the United States Government

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#3035. Chiefs-Jets Madden Simulation Week 9: Get Right Week

On Sunday, the (5-2) Kansas City Chiefs are back at Arrowhead taking on the (2-6) New York Jets. Let's see how the game plays out in Madden NFL 27.

Note: Madden simulation is 6-1 this season, predicting the outcome.

If you want to watch the simulation without spoilers, start this video — or if you can't see it, click here.

The Kansas City Chiefs received the opening kickoff and wasted no time getting the offense going.

At midfield, Patrick Mahomes lobbed a pass down the left sideline to Kenneth Walker, who was running a wheel route. Walker hauled it in and took it all the way to the Jets' four-yard line.

The New York Jets defense attempted to stand tall, but on third-and-four, Mahomes found Travis Kelce on a slant route in the end zone for a touchdown.

The Jets could potentially welcome back running back Breece Hall after his quad injury. On their first five plays, Hall had his hands on the football. His biggest play came on a 16-yard run straight up the middle.

Felix Anudike-Uzomah got to Geno Smith for a sack, forcing the Jets to punt.

The Chiefs picked up one first down but couldn't get past midfield. Matt Araiza came on to punt the ball back to the Jets.

The Chiefs defense quickly got the ball back as Chris Jones and George Karlaftis teamed up to sack Geno Smith on third down, forcing a three-and-out.

Mahomes connected with Kelce twice for 37 yards, moving the Chiefs inside the five-yard line. After a play that resulted in a 10-yard loss, Mahomes went right back to Kelce in the end zone for his second touchdown of the game.

At their own 24-yard line, Hall took a handoff straight up the middle, broke a tackle, and made several defenders miss on his way to the end zone. A 76-yard touchdown run brought the Jets back into the game.

Rashee Rice picked up a first down on a drag route to midfield. On the very next play, Mahomes delivered a strike to Xavier Worthy, who ran a corner route before cutting upfield and racing into the end zone for a 50-yard touchdown.

Jadon Canady intercepted Smith with 49 seconds remaining at the Jets' 44-yard line.

The Chiefs failed to capitalize on the opportunity before halftime.

The Jets quickly went three-and-out. During the drive, Mansoor Delane came screaming off the edge on a blitz and brought down Smith for a sack.

The Chiefs controlled the majority of the quarter, putting together a lengthy drive deep into Jets territory. At the 10-yard line, Mahomes kept the ball on a read option and took a massive hit before being brought down at the two-yard line.

On third-and-goal from the two, Walker caught a screen pass from Mahomes and reached the end zone for a touchdown, giving the Chiefs a three-score lead.

Karlaftis got to Smith for another sack, forcing the Jets to punt once again.

On the first play of the quarter, Walker broke free and reached the Jets' 16-yard line.

Worthy followed it up by catching a touchdown pass in the end zone, giving the Chiefs a four-possession lead.

The Jets managed to put together a 12-play scoring drive, capped off by an Adonai Mitchell touchdown.

New York attempted an onside kick, but Jalen Royals had other plans. Royals recovered the football and returned it all the way to the four-yard line.

However, Mahomes threw an interception, giving the Jets possession once again.

Facing fourth-and-goal from the nine-yard line, Smith lobbed a pass toward the corner of the end zone, where Tim Patrick hauled it in for a touchdown.

Patrick Mahomes: 26 for 30 passing, 320 yards, 5 touchdowns, 1 interception, 5 rushes, 21 yards.

Kenneth Walker: 9 rushing attempts, 78 yards, 8 receptions, 88 yards, 1 touchdown.

Travis Kelce: 6 receptions, 87 yards, 2 touchdowns.

Xavier Worthy: 4 receptions, 71 yards, 2 touchdowns.

Geno Smith: 15 for 23 passing, 175 yards, 2 touchdowns, 1 interception.

Breece Hall: 12 rushing attempts, 119 yards, 1 touchdown, 4 receptions, 30 yards

Adonai Mitchell: 4 receptions, 53 yards, 1 touchdown

Next week, the Chiefs are back on the road heading to Hotlanta to visit the Atlanta Falcons. Until then, we will see how it is played out inside the EA Universe.

#3030. Report: Warriors contact Bulls, Pelicans, and Bucks about Kristaps Porzingis trade

The Golden State Warriors have contacted the New Orleans Pelicans, Chicago Bulls, and Milwaukee Bucks to gauge the organization's interest in a potential trade for center Kristaps Porzingis (or really his contract), per a report by Brett Siegel of ClutchPoints. With Porzingis likely headed for retirement and accepting the Warriors withholding his salary, Siegel reports that he is expected to accept a significant buyout from his $20 million salary. The Dubs front office is trying to use his contract as a trade exception in the next month to solidify the team's depth without limiting their options at the trade deadline.

"Among the many teams the Warriors have contacted since the start of the season, as reported earlier on Friday in our weekly newsletter, ClutchPoints has been able to confirm with league sources three teams in particular: the Milwaukee Bucks, Chicago Bulls, and New Orleans Pelicans," Siegel wrote.

The Warriors interest in Pelicans wings Trey Murphy III and Herb Jones has been reported on for years, and the Dubs reportedly called New Orleans about center Yves Missi earlier this season as well. However, the fact that the two sides have not previously agreed to a deal should leave fans skeptical that a deal will happen now (especially months before the trade deadline).

Siegel's reporting suggests the Warriors and Bucks were not close to a deal for Myles Turner, with different evaluations of his contract's value. As for the Bulls, Siegel highlighted center Jalen Smith.

"Going back to the topic of potential trade suitors for Porzingis, the Warriors showed interest in Bulls' center Jalen Smith in a potential Porzingis-oriented deal with Chicago, but the Bulls have not shown a willingness to make such a move at this time, sources said," Siegel said. "The Bulls are wanting to evaluate their roster and potential moves closer to the trade deadline unless draft compensation is included, despite opening the year as one of the worst teams in the NBA."

Smith would be an excellent to the Warriors current front court depth. While he lacks the explosive ceiling that Golden State hoped to get from Porzingis, he is an incredibly well rounded role player who would give head coach Steve Kerr another center who can shoot threes other than 40-year old Al Horford.

The 10th overall pick in the 2020 NBA Draft by the Phoenix Suns, Smith never found his footing in Phoenix and was quickly viewed around the league as a bust before finding consistency with the Indiana Pacers. Over the past five seasons, split between Indiana and Chicago, Smith has averaged 9.4 points, 5.9 rebounds, and 0.8 blocks on 50.1%/34.3%/74.9% shooting in 18.0 minutes per game (254 games, 76 starts).

Still only 26, Smith has been the primary starting center for the first time in his career this season. While he is posting career highs in minutes (22.5), points (11.0), rebounds (7.8), assists (1.8), and blocks (0.9) per game so far this season, the Bulls are 1-7 and have been among the worst teams in the league. Slated to be a free agent at the end of the season, Smith seems destined to be traded before the deadline for a package built around second-round picks. The question is, will the Warriors be able to convince the Bulls to make that move before the new year?

The Warriors only have two tradeable second round picks, one in 2030 and another in 2033. One added enticement they could add to a deal with the Bulls, who have notoriously cheap owner Jerry Reinsdorf, would be to take back either Buddy Hield or Zach Collins alongside Smith. The Warriors would likely waive either player, but would effectively be taking $18-$19 million off the Bulls books. Assuming the Bulls agreed to a buyout with Porzingis in the $6-8 million range that Siegel speculates, Bulls ownership could save around $10 million.

#3027. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3024. Broncos release S Taylor Rapp, but he will re-sign to the practice squad tomorrow

According to 9NEWS Denver's Mike Klis, the Denver Broncos released veteran safety Taylor Rapp. However, this is just a procedural move to open up a 53-man roster spot, and Rapp is expected to be signed back to the practice squad tomorrow and likely elevated to the game-day roster on Sunday.

With Pat Bryant expected to be activated off the injured reserve tomorrow, the team needed to open up a roster spot for their talented wide receiver.

With starting safety Brandon Jones ruled OUT and Talanoa Hufanga listed as questionable, it's possible we still see Rapp elevated to the game-day roster on Sunday. However, the Broncos also ruled out defensive lineman Eyioma Uwazurike and cornerback Kris Abrams-Draine, so they have some decisions to make when they announce which two players they are elevating from the practice squad tomorrow.

With Jones ruled out and Hufanga questionable, we likely see Rapp get the start on Sunday at one of the safety positions or at least see some significant playing time in the Broncos' secondary. Devon Key has seen some playing time while Hufanga and Jones have been sidelined, and JL Skinner made a strong case for some added playing time after his big hit on Chiefs running back Kenneth Walker at the goal line during the Broncos ' goal-line stand last Sunday. So, we will have to see how they distribute the playing time this Sunday vs. the Panthers.

Rapp's roster spot was needed for second-year wide receiver Pat Bryant, who is expected to be activated from the injured reserve tomorrow. Bryant will be a major boost to the Broncos' offense and will give quarterback Bo Nix yet another talented pass-catcher to target vs. the Panthers. This will also be Rapp's third practice squad elevation this season, so if they want to use him again after Sunday, they will need to re-sign him to the 53-man roster.

Stay tuned to Mile High Report tomorrow as we'll update you on Pat Bryant's likely activation from the injured reserve, and we'll have the practice squad elevation news for you as well.

#3023. Detroit Lions Make Amon-Ra St. Brown Announcement Before Dolphins Game

The Detroit Lions have five players ruled out against the Miami Dolphins. They still haven't settled the status of one of their most important offensive weapons.

Amon-Ra St. Brown is questionable for Sunday's game with a rib injury, according to Detroit's final injury report released Friday, Nov. 6. The wide receiver remained limited throughout the week, leaving Jared Goff's offense waiting on a consequential availability decision.

St. Brown has offered a more encouraging assessment. But his confidence and the team's official designation are two different pieces of the picture heading into Miami.

Speaking with reporters Thursday, St. Brown acknowledged the injury without suggesting he expected it to keep him off the field.

"I'm a little banged up, but I'll be alright," St. Brown said, according to Pride of Detroit's Erik Schlitt.

Friday's report stopped short of clearing him. St. Brown received a limited designation Wednesday, Thursday and Friday, although Detroit noted that Wednesday's walkthrough participation was estimated.

That leaves room for optimism without turning his comments into a guarantee. He participated during the week, but he never advanced to a full designation.

There is also a distinction from the injury Detroit managed before its previous game. Heavy previously covered St. Brown's ankle issue following the Green Bay Packers matchup. This week's report identifies his rib.

His recent production underscores the stakes: St. Brown caught seven passes for 46 yards against Minnesota after a nine-catch, 153-yard game against Green Bay, per NFL.com.

For Detroit, replacing that volume would mean redistributing a substantial part of the passing game. Even if St. Brown plays, his designation alone offers no answer about how many snaps he will handle.

St. Brown's status is the unresolved offensive headline on a report already filled with confirmed absences.

Linebacker Jack Campbell (neck), safety Kerby Joseph (knee), center Cade Mays (ankle), cornerback D.J. Reed (hip) and edge rusher DJ Wonnum (concussion) are out. Detroit's mounting practice absences have now translated into game-day losses across several position groups.

Running backs Jacob Saylors (neck) and Sione Vaki (ankle) join St. Brown as questionable. Vaki was added Friday and did not practice, creating another availability question late in the week.

Brian Branch provides a brighter development. The defensive back progressed from limited participation Wednesday to full participation Thursday and Friday and received no game-status designation. Neither did linebacker Amen Ogbongbemiga or cornerback Rock Ya-Sin.

Detroit has some answers on defense. Its biggest remaining question on offense is whether St. Brown's confidence becomes a place in Sunday's lineup.

Klay Thompson looking at at fan after Steph Curry pointed at him. (X/@NBA).

#3021. 'The whole landscape changed': Gaming CEOs see new opportunities post-midterms

Midterm election results have created new openings for U.S. gambling expansion, while raising the prospect of greater political and legal pressure on prediction markets, according to gaming industry executives.

"The whole landscape changed," said Craig Billings, CEO of Wynn Resorts, during the company's earnings call Thursday.

Wynn has been closely watchng developments in the Middle East as it looks to open the United Arab Emirates' first legal casino in 2027. Billings said he suspects the election will have an impact on the "likely trajectory of the conflict in the region."

Closer to home, Billings sees opportunities in Texas. And he's not the only one. On the heels of reporting record third-quarter revenue and raising guidance for its brick-and-mortar business, Penn Entertainment CEO Jay Snowden called out specific opportunities made possible by state leadership changes.

Election Day was "a very good day overall for the industry, and certainly for Penn," Snowden said on Thursday's earnings call.

Texas, in particular, could be a jackpot for the casino industry. In a massive upset, Democrat Vikki Goodwin defeated longtime Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who has been persistent in blocking gambling expansion in the Lone Star State.

"Texas is a fascinating market," Billings said, explaining how many consumers travel to Oklahoma to gamble at tribal casions. "So it's kind of a stealth market that is giant."

Penn has been building a position in Texas for years, Snowden said. It operates two of the state's three Class 1 thoroughbred racetracks and has established relationships with local leaders.

Penn plans to spend more time in Texas and, if lawmakers move toward legalization, Snowden said he expects the company is “positioned as well, if not better than really anybody in the sector.”

Las Vegas Sands also has invested heavily for many years to get casino gambling legalized in Texas.

"When Las Vegas Sands thinks about Texas and the Metroplex, it's really about an asset that will be both for business and future tourism," Sands Chairman and CEO Patrick Dumont told CNBC in an interview in October in Macao.

Dumont recently acquired the controlling stake in the Dallas Mavericks from his mother-in-law Miriam Adelson. He said there is the potential for a new Mavericks arena to incorporate a future casino.

"Whatever Las Vegas Sands can do in the future, that will be its own location, its own site and its own timeline," he said.

Houston Rockets owner Tilman Fertitta, currently serving as U.S. ambassador to Italy, owns Golden Nugget casinos, is Wynn Resorts' largest investor and is buying Caesars to take it private. Following a CNBC interview in Italy, he said, "Of course I want gaming in Texas!"

A new market would draw national sportsbook operators that would likely include DraftKings, Flutter-owned FanDuel, Caesars, Fanatics, and BetMGM, which is co-owned by MGM and Entain, among others.

Though the Texas' state election removed a major political obstacle, it didn't produce a deal. Legalizing casino gambling or sports betting would still require a constitutional amendment approved by two-thirds of both chambers and then voters.

Texas state control is now divided. While Democrats flipped the state House, Republicans kept the Senate. Democrat Gina Hinojosa won the governorship.

Las Vegas Sands spokesman Ron Reese told CNBC it's too early to speculate about what might happen with Texas gaming, but that the company will monitor developments closely.

Truist gaming analyst Barry Jonas wrote in a Wednesday note that he sees mixed implications from the midterm results.

"The Ohio governor-elect Amy Acton's support for regulated online casinos could improve the chances of iGaming legalization," Jonas said. On the other hand, Nebraska voters rejected ballot measures to legalize online betting.

Georgia, where Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governorship, could also provide an opportunity. She campaigned on letting voters decide whether to legalize casino gambling, with revenue expected to be earmarked to help fund education. Sports betting and casinos remain illegal in the state, and earlier sports-betting legislation failed in the House.

Penn could also benefit if lawmakers seek tighter regulations for so-called skill-based gaming machines, which compete with casinos and licensed video gaming terminals, according to Snowden. He said candidates who support skill-based gaming machines lost seats or influence in several states.

Penn operates four casinos in Pennsylvania as well as video gaming terminals in that state and other jurisdictions, and has three casinos in Missouri.

Snowden linked recent strong year-over-year gaming revenue growth to the shutdown of skill games in Pennsylvania in mid-October. Although he cautioned that it was too early to know whether Pennsylvania’s growth would last, he noted Missouri also saw strong revenue growth as its state attorney general pursued the machines there.

In Indiana, voters in three counties rejected a proposed casino license, which Penn sees as a benefit as it limits new competition for existing casinos in Indiana and neighboring Illinois and Ohio. Some gaming insiders speculate that a wave of left-leaning progressives in power could work to limit gambling businesses.

"There's no shortage of noise about where this industry is headed, and the election certainly turns up the volume," said Joe Maloney, president of Sports Betting Alliance. "What hasn't changed with the election is what works, and that's state-regulated sports betting and iGaming, built on accountability, consumer protection, and tax revenue for states. That's our lane, and we'll make that case to every new lawmaker willing to listen."

Democratic victories are likely to increase the pressure on prediction markets, even as industry supporters call out their predictive value in forecasting the election results.

"Every eligible customer should have access to these markets, which are federally regulated through our CFTC-registered Futures Commission Merchant," a Robinhood spokesperson said.

Democrats’ control of Congress gives them subpoena power and committee resources to investigate the president’s family and business dealings. That could put Donald Trump Jr. back in the spotlight as he is a paid strategic adviser to Kalshi and sits on Polymarket’s advisory board.

Congress could also consider legislation limiting the markets’ scope. The bipartisan Prediction Markets Are Gambling Act would bar Commodity Futures Trading Commission-regulated exchanges from offering contracts based on sports or casino-style games. But the bill had only been referred to committee as of the election. A veto-proof law would require two-thirds support in both chambers; no such vote is currently scheduled.

The American Gaming Association has been fighting prediction markets as unlicensed gambling.

"The prediction markets are facing a tsunami of criminal charges, major civil investigations and significant defeats in court," Bill Miller, the trade group's CEO, told CNBC. "In the aftermath of the election, we expect the bipartisan scrutiny of these platforms to further intensify."

"Our platform is an institutional-grade exchange with strong market-integrity and customer-protection programs," Polymarket told CNBC. A spokesman said the company welcomes the opportunity to work with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle.

Repeated requests to Kalshi for comment went unanswered.

Jeff LeRiche, a partner at law firm Husch Blackwell, said he expects more scrutiny.

"Entities with robust compliance and surveillance infrastructure will be well positioned to navigate the increased attention," said LeRiche, who spent more than 20 years in the CFTC's enforcement division.

The state-level legal fight is already underway. Utah has filed criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit rejected Kalshi’s request for a rehearing in its Nevada case, leaving the company exposed to state enforcement there.

New Democratic attorneys general in Ohio, Michigan and Nevada will also decide how to handle inherited disputes.

Ohio’s outgoing AG Andy Wilson helped lead a multistate effort to defend state gambling laws, Michigan’s Dana Nessel sued Kalshi and won a preliminary injunction, and Nevada’s Aaron Ford secured injunctions against prediction-market operators.

New AGs could also decide to follow Utah's lead in filing criminal charges, along with Arizona where criminal charges are pending.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#3020. NFL news, live updates: Rams' Myles Garrett reportedly in line to return Sunday; Shedeur Sanders to start for Browns with Deshaun Watson in concussion protocol

We're officially into Week 9 of the NFL season (and the looming NFL trade deadline), where teams will have to make a decision on the intended course of the remainder of their season — are they going all in? Or is it time to tear it all down and look toward the future?

NFL fans also await news on the fate of star running back Jonathan Taylor, who has been touted as a potential trade-deadline mover as the Indianapolis Colts kicked off their franchise reset with a fire sale. They've already traded away two foundational players, Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, for several draft picks as they look to the future, leaving us to wonder whether Taylor could be next.

The Los Angeles Rams could be a deadline buyer, as they've won back-to-back games and are reportedly already getting a long-awaited midseason reinforcement in the form of edge rusher Myles Garrett, a two-time NFL Defensive Player of the Year and the league's single-season sack record holder.

Garrett's in line for a return to game action on the road Sunday against the Washington Commanders, according to NFL Network's Mike Garafolo and Ian Rapoport. Coming back from early-season knee surgery that landed him on injured reserve, Garrett has practiced all week.

Shedeur Sanders will start at quarterback for the Cleveland Browns in Week 9 against the New Orleans Saints, head coach Todd Monken announced.

Deshaun Watson returned to practice in a limited capacity on Friday, but remains in concussion protocol. Monken said it's unclear if Watson will serve as the backup this week, though he will be listed as questionable

The Browns' No. 1 quarterback took a helmet-to-helmet hit during the first quarter of Sunday's loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers. Watson missed only four snaps, but later reported experiencing concussion symptoms on Monday night.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more ahead of Week 9.

The Seattle Seahawks sent a pair of draft picks to the Indianapolis Colts in exchange for six-time All-Pro guard Quenton Nelson.

After just one game in a Seahawks uniform, Nelson is now out.

He's "week-to-week," according to Seattle head coach Mike Macdonald, who told reporters Friday that the 30-year-old offensive lineman needs a minor knee procedure.

"We're not going to put him on IR," Macdonald said, via The Seattle Times' Bob Condotta.

When asked what happened to Nelson, Macdonald said the interior o-lineman had something he was dealing with when he arrived.

"We re-imaged it, and it was aggravated to some degree, so we've kind of done the due diligence, and everybody agrees it's best to take care of it now and play the long game."

Notably, the Seahawks will also be down quarterback Sam Darnold (quad) this week against the Arizona Cardinals. Backup Drew Lock will start in his place.

Detroit Lions safety Kerby Joseph is out for Week 9's matchup with the Miami Dolphins. However, Joseph took first-team reps as a limited participant in practice all week and "looked awesome," according to Lions safeties coach Jim O'Neil.

"The focus right now is let's see if we can stack some days," O'Neil told the Detroit Free Press' Dave Birkett. "Let's see if he can look like he looked yesterday for three or four days in a row."

Joseph, who turns 26 on Nov. 14, hasn't played for the Lions since Week 6 of last season due to a cartilage condition in his knee. He was an All-Pro selection in 2024, his third NFL season, compiling 9 interceptions and 83 tackles.

The Lions signed Joseph to a four-year extension in April 2025 that made him the highest-paid safety in the NFL at the time.

Injuries continue to plague the Eagles, who will again be without top receivers DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks this week against the Giants. The team has officially ruled out both wideouts for Sunday's game, after neither practiced on Friday.

Both Smith, who has been dealing with a hamstring injury, and Wicks, who has an ankle injury, have missed two games with the injury, and are set to now miss a third.

However, there's a bit of good news: Saquon Barkley, who was listed as limited in practice with hamstring and back injuries, should be clear to play on Sunday.

Kansas City Chiefs head coach Andy Reid told reporters Friday that rookies Peter Woods and Mansoor Delane won't play Sunday against the New York Jets.

"They're making progress, so that's a positive thing," Reid said of the defensive tackle and cornerback, per ESPN's Nate Taylor.

Woods is dealing with a neck injury, and Delane is navigating a shoulder issue. Delane, this year's No. 6 overall pick, hasn't played since a Week 7 loss to the Seattle Seahawks. Woods, whom the Chiefs selected 29th overall back in April, suffered his setback last week in a defeat to the Denver Broncos.

The New England Patriots have ruled out both wide receiver A.J. Brown (ankle/knee) and running back TreVeyon Henderson (illness) for Sunday's game against the Green Bay Packers.

They've listed tight end Hunter Henry — the team's receptions leader — as "questionable" with a foot issue.

Brown, whom the Patriots gave up a pair of picks for (including a 2028 first-rounder), has appeared in only three games this season. He suffered a high ankle sprain in the season opener and noted late last month that he wasn't 100 percent upon returning to game action.

As for Henderson, the second-year back revealed on Friday that he was hospitalized after experiencing "unexplained neurological symptoms."

Although the results of Henderson's examination were "positive and reassuring," doctors have yet to determine a cause, Henderson explained in his social media post.

Watson returned to practice in a limited capacity on Friday, but remains in concussion protocol. Monken said it's unclear if Watson will serve as the backup this week, though he will be listed as questionable

The Browns' No. 1 quarterback took a helmet-to-helmet hit during the first quarter of Sunday's loss to the Pittsburgh Steelers. Watson missed only four snaps, but later reported experiencing concussion symptoms on Monday night.

The two-time NFL Defensive Player of the Year, who's also the league's single-season sack record holder, went under the knife in the wake of the Rams' season-opening defeat to the San Francisco 49ers in Melbourne, Australia.

Watson entered concussion protocol earlier this week after reporting symptoms on symptoms on Monday night. Shedeur Sanders has worked with the first team to prepare in case Watson was ruled out.

Henderson says he was examined, and while the results were "positive and reassuring," doctors have yet to determine a cause.

While the results of my comprehensive medical testing have come back positive and reassuring, the cause of this remains unknown and unexplainable.

I am committed to doing everything necessary to return to full health. My health and long-term well-being remain my foremost priorities."

For the second straight day, the Patriots have designated wide receiver A.J. Brown and running back TreVeyon Henderson as DNP, leaving their offensive production in doubt ahead of Sunday's game against the Packers.

Brown, who returned from a high ankle sprain in Week 6, said last week that he's been "pushing through" the injury. He is listed as dealing with ankle and knee injuries.

TreVeyon Henderson is again listed as being out with an illness, though the team has not elaborated on his condition. Last month, Henderson was expected to be ruled out multiple weeks with a high ankle sprain, but continued playing for two more weeks before missing last week's game against the Dolphins.

Los Angeles Rams cornerback Trent McDuffie appears to be sitting out his second consecutive day of practice with a back injury suffered in Week 8.

In six games this season, his first with the Rams, McDuffie has 25 tackles, 2 interceptions and 8 passes defended.

Perhaps looking ahead to McDuffie being unavailable for Week 9, the Rams signed veteran David Long to their practice squad, The Athletic's Nate Atkins reports.

Long, 28, has played seven NFL seasons, most recently with the Carolina Panthers last year. During his career, he has averaged 20 tackles and 2 passes defended per season. Long was a 2019 third-round pick by the Rams.

Although Jacksonville Jaguars running back Bhayshul Tuten is "questionable" with ankle and foot issues, he "should be" good for Thursday night's game versus the Baltimore Ravens — that is, barring any pregame setbacks — according to ESPN's Jeremy Fowler.

#3019. Kaufman-Renn scores 17 and No. 17 Kansas routs Middle Tennessee 89-45

LAWRENCE, Kan. (AP) — Trey Kaufman-Renn scored 17 points in 18 minutes and led No. 17 Kansas to an 89-45 rout of Middle Tennessee on Friday night.

Kaufman-Renn, a fifth-year senior who transferred from Purdue, shot 7 for 8 from the field and grabbed four rebounds. Through two games with Kansas (2-0), he is 11 for 13 from the field.

His efficiency helped pace the Jayhawks, who shot a blistering 63% in the game while holding Middle Tennessee to just 25%.

Fifth-year center Christian Reeves added 10 points for Kansas.

The Jayhawks were particularly effective in the paint, using their size to outscore the Blue Raiders 48-16. They also pulled down 32 defensive rebounds to limit second chances.

The Kansas bench chipped in 46 points to help keep Middle Tennessee at bay and build the lead to as many as 50 points in the second half.

Middle Tennessee (1-1) had one player, Eli Rice, finish in double figures. He led the team with 11 points.

The Jayhawks got off to a hot start and built a 36-14 lead by halftime. Their stifling defense limited the Blue Raiders to just 18.8% from the field in the first period and forced 10 turnovers.

Going into the final television timeout before the break, the Blue Raiders had 12 points and 12 fouls.

Kansas converted 16 Middle Tennessee turnovers into 28 points.

Middle Tennessee: Begins a two-game homestand against Evansville on Tuesday night.

Kansas: Travels to Chicago to face Kentucky in the State Farm Champions Classic on Tuesday night.

#3018. Westchester Knicks 2026-27 Season Primer

New York's G League affiliate opens its 2026-27 season tonight against the Maine Celtics in the Tip-Off Tournament. The roster features three players on two-way contracts, a returning fan favorite (hint: he's great on toast), and a collection of prospects hoping to dribble their way up to an NBA court.

With the Knicks operating near the second apron, Westchester provides a way to develop young players without committing additional standard roster spots. Two-way players can spend most of the season in White Plains while remaining available for NBA duty, subject to a limit of up to 50 games on the active roster. The G League has also given players like Deuce McBride and Tyler Kolek opportunities to get more work. Here's a peek at the players worth watching.

Of everyone on Westchester's roster, Dalton Knecht has the clearest path to helping the big club this season. The 2024 first-round pick (17th overall) arrived after the Lakers waived him, with New York releasing Kevin McCullar Jr. to make room for Knecht on a two-way contract. This low-risk move could pay off if the former Tennessee star rediscovers his shooting touch and improves his court awareness, which was a criticism raised in the comments section at Silver Screen and Roll.

The 25-year-old made a promising debut for the New York Knicks this week, scoring 10 points on efficient 4-of-6 shooting (including two threes) with seven boards, a dime, and a block in 16 minutes off the bench in a rout of the Bulls. Finding consistent playing time will be difficult, however. With Mikal Bridges, OG Anunoby, Josh Hart, Landry Shamet, Pacôme Dadiet, and Mohamed Diawara competing for minutes on the wings, Knecht will probably need an injury or another opening to receive an extended look. (TBH, he already looks ahead of Dadiet.)

Coach Mike Brown has said he'd rather see Knecht playing meaningful minutes in the G League than sitting on the NBA bench. Makes sense. Knecht needs opportunities to shoot, make decisions, and work through mistakes. He has shown he can score, but questions remain about his defense, shot selection, and ability to contribute consistently as a role player.

If Knecht performs well enough, the organization could eventually consider converting him to a standard NBA contract, which would require sufficient roster and financial room. For now, he should get plenty of opportunities to let it fly against G League competition.

Speaking of flamethrowers . . . Tyler "Silent K" Nickel might be the most straightforward fit for what the Knicks need. New York selected the Vanderbilt sharpshooter with the 47th pick in the 2026 NBA Draft after an impressive senior season. He followed that with a promising Summer League showing and signed a two-way contract.

From what we've seen, Nickel moves well without the ball, finds open space, and punishes defenses for leaving him unattended. The big league Knicks have several players capable of creating offense, and a dependable perimeter shooter who doesn't require many touches could fit nicely alongside them (see: Landry Shamet).

His defense will help determine how quickly the 23-year-old advances. He needs to prove he can handle quicker, stronger NBA bodies while maintaining his shooting efficiency against more aggressive closeouts. Westchester should give him plenty of reps to work on both.

Nickel doesn't seem to have Knecht's offensive versatility, but his shooting gives him a clearly defined role. If he can hold his own defensively, he could become a useful option for additional spacing. For the time being, Westchester might have its very own set of Splash Brothers to keep you entertained (at a reasonable price).

Jaden Akins took a less direct route to New York. Undrafted out of Michigan State in 2025, he spent last season with the Motor City Cruise after earning All-Big Ten third-team and All-Defensive recognition as a senior. His combination of scoring, secondary playmaking, and defense caught the Knicks' attention. Westchester acquired his returning player rights in September, and he secured a two-way contract before the season.

Akins faces considerable competition at the one- and two-guard spots. His best chance of separating himself is to use his time at Westchester to polish his defense and his work off the ball. Can he defend multiple positions, run an offense for stretches, and make open shots? Can he contribute consistently without committing unnecessary fouls or turnovers? Those qualities will probably matter more to New York than his individual scoring averages.

The fan favorite returns! Dink Pate returns to Westchester after a confusing offseason. The 6'8" wing, still only 20 years old, averaged approximately 16 points, seven rebounds, and four assists across 50 games with the club last season. He subsequently attempted to play college basketball at Providence, but that plan fell through amid questions about his NCAA eligibility. The Knicks signed and waived him on an Exhibit 10 contract, and he returned to Westchester, which already held his G League rights.

Pate has length, can create his own offense, and contributes as a rebounder and passer. His combination of physical tools and professional experience makes him one of Westchester's more interesting developmental players. Decision-making and efficiency are where he needs improvement.

Unlike Knecht, Nickel, and Akins, Pate doesn't have a two-way contract. He would need an available two-way or standard roster spot and a new NBA contract to join the big club. Westchester's rights also don't prevent another NBA team from signing him. Thus, his time in White Plains is a prove-it run for a broader audience—so we expect to see some inspired hooping from him this season.

Westchester's frontcourt includes several players who could provide useful depth. Liam Robbins, a seven-footer who went undrafted out of Vanderbilt in 2023, is probably the most recognizable. He showed some encouraging work around the iron during Summer League with the Knicks, and Westchester subsequently acquired his G League rights.

Robbins brings size, rebounding, and shot-blocking ability. The big league club could use more reliable center depth behind Karl-Anthony Towns and Andre Drummond, with James Wiseman slow to make an impression. Robbins could use his time in White Plains to convince the team he deserves a look—perhaps before the front office starts eyeing its surplus of guards as trade bait.

N'Faly Dante, the 6-foot-11 former Oregon center, is working his way back from an ACL injury. His size and athleticism lift our eyebrows, but getting healthy and regaining his previous form will be the immediate priorities.

Igor Miličić Jr., a 6-foot-10 forward out of Tennessee, brings international experience and positional versatility after spending time with the Delaware Blue Coats last season. Improving his outside shooting and defensive consistency would help him take the next step.

Westchester also has several forwards competing for rotation minutes. Nick Jourdain returns after averaging roughly eight points and more than five rebounds last season. The former Memphis forward provides rebounding, energy, and defensive effort. His journey from local tryout to returning player is a nice story, even if an NBA opportunity remains a longer shot.

Toby Okani, the former West Virginia wing, brings length and athleticism after spending time with multiple G League organizations. David Dixon, an undrafted forward out of Duquesne, and Tahj-Malik Campbell are also trying to establish roles after joining the training camp group.

Westchester's backcourt includes several capable players. Mitchell Mascari is worth watching after arriving from the Stockton Kings. The 6'5" guard shot approximately 42% from downtown last season, giving Westchester another option to stretch the floor.

Paul Zilinskas returns as a scoring option at guard and on the wing, while Amir "Primo" Spears joins the club after being selected 25th in the 2026 G League Draft. Spears brings extensive college experience, including a productive stint at UTSA, and spent last season playing professionally in Mexico.

The three two-way players will receive the most attention this season, although we'll also keep an eye on Pate ('cuz he's kinda electric). The rest of the roster will compete for minutes and try to establish themselves as dependable professional players. Westchester opens its Tip-Off Tournament schedule tonight at 7 p.m. against the Maine Celtics at the Portland Expo, then faces the same opponent at the same venue Sunday at 12:30 p.m. Both games will air on MSG Network.

#3017. Kyle Shanahan on Brandon Aiyuk's meeting with 49ers: I couldn't care less

49ers team officials will meet with wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk and his agent, Drew Rosenhaus, at the team's business offices in an hour. Coach Kyle Shanahan said it has nothing to do with him.

"No clue. Your guess is as good as mine," Shanahan told reporters Friday when asked what he expected out of the meeting. "I [couldn't] care less."

Aiyuk last played in a game on Oct. 20, 2024, when he tore the ACL and MCL in his right knee. The relationship between the team and the player fell apart after that, and the 49ers voided the guaranteed money due to his unwillingness to rehab at their facility.

The 49ers placed him on the reserve/left squad list on Dec. 13, 2025. Rosenhaus filed a reinstatement request on Oct. 27, but the NFL still hasn't reinstated him.

The only certainty is Aiyuk will never play for the 49ers again.

"It's been over for our football team for a long time," Shanahan said. "So, I don't hear much except questions about him today, but not too big of a deal for me to answer.

"I look at this as zero to do with our football team. Anything that has zero to do with our football team, especially during the season, I will give zero thoughts to."

#3016. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3015. Jordan Walsh reflects on his “okay” start to the season

CHARLOTTE — Jordan Walsh isn't thrilled with his start to the season. The 22-year-old has played double-digit minutes in every game and has been tasked with guarding the opposing team's best players, as is customary.

But at Celtics shootaround Friday morning, Walsh said he was underwhelmed with his impact on the team so far.

"I think it's been okay," Walsh said. "I don't think it's been good or great or nothing like that. I think it's been just okay. Haven't been, like, the best version of myself, but the team's winning, everybody's happy, so it's all good."

Walsh started the first five games of the season but has come off the bench for the last three. He hasn't shot particularly well — he's shooting 38.2% from the field and 30% from three — but it's all in a very small sample. So far, he's averaging 4.3 points and 3.8 rebounds in 15.4 minutes per night, both numbers slightly lower than last season.

"I haven't been playing great — but the starting, not starting thing, it's not about that," Walsh said. "That's just kind of like Joe's thing, like he wants to try new things. Like, that's fine. But I'm still playing the same amount of minutes. So, I still gotta produce in those minutes. Like, I gotta do that better."

Walsh isn't on the court to stuff the stat sheet; his defensive success sometimes shows up as steals or blocks, but more often, it's more nuanced.

"For me, it's more about the defensive impact, and a lot of the time that stuff doesn't really show up in the box score," Walsh said. "Like, it doesn't show up how many times I negate a screen, or how many times the star player doesn't get the ball that possession, just gives up. Like, it doesn't track that kind of stuff. So it's never really based off of that, anyway — that's not where my value lies."

Walsh's minutes have been trending downwards as Hugo Gonzalez has slotted into the starting lineup. After playing 18 or more minutes in three of the Celtics' first four games, he hasn't played more than 12 minutes since October 27th.

While he knows his counting stats aren't the priority, he said he wants to see a marked improvement in his defensive impact. It's an imperfect stat, especially this early on, but he currently has the second-worst plus-minus on the team; the Celtics have been outscored by 10 points in his minutes so far this season (Mitchell Robinson has the worst total plus-minus on the team at -37).

"I still have a standard that I've set, and the organization has for me that I need to meet, even if it's not scoring."
