#3013. Family offices boost startup investments in October, backing women's health and caregivers

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

After a slow September, investment firms of ultra-wealthy families stepped up their deal-making last month. In October, family offices made 56 direct investments in companies, up about 30% from the month prior, according to data provided exclusively to CNBC by Fintrx, a private wealth intelligence platform.

Laurene Powell Jobs' Emerson Collective was the most prolific investor of the month, backing three healthcare startups according to Fintrx data. Her investment and philanthropy firm seeks to address health equity and other complex challenges like the energy transition.

Last month, Emerson Collective joined a $555 million Series G for Devoted Health, a healthcare provider and insurer of Medicare Advantage plans, alongside information technology billionaire Azim Premji's namesake family office. Devoted Health, which has counted Emerson Collective as an investor since 2021, announced that it will use the fundraise to expand to five additional states for a total of 34.

Powell Jobs' firm also participated in two fundraises for healthcare data startups BioStack and Collective X Health. BioStack, which raised $9.5 million in seed funding, analyzes patient records across different institutions to create training datasets for artificial intelligence healthcare models. Collective X Health, fresh off a $3.2 million pre-seed round, seeks to close the gender data gap by compiling and licensing peer-reviewed, female-specific data and research.

Emerson Collective is in good company as family offices continue to invest in healthcare innovations from telehealth to drug discovery. In a family office survey released by Citi Wealth in September, healthcare ranked as the second-most-popular sector for direct investing at 32%, only behind AI at 51%.

Melinda French Gates' Pivotal Ventures joined an $82 million Series C for Abby Care, an AI-enabled startup that trains and certifies family members as paid caregivers under Medicaid and Medicare programs.

Erin Harkless Moore, who manages investments at Pivotal Ventures, told CNBC that the firm looks for founders who have first-hand experience with the complexities of healthcare. Abby Care CEO and founder Havi Nguyen started navigating the Medicaid system at age 9 as a caregiver for her sisters and eventually her grandparents.

"We look for teams that understand the problem deeply and have a model built for the realities of the healthcare system as it currently exists. Abby Care is a great example," Moore said. "Their deep understanding of the market allowed them to both spot an unsolved problem and understand how to navigate the regulatory challenges in this space."

For family offices looking to invest in healthcare, Moore said overlooked markets offer a chance to back companies addressing needs that have long gone unmet.

"Women's health is a space that has been historically underfunded and under-researched. We have less data, less funding, and huge gaps to fill," she said. "That can make investing more challenging, but it can also point to where there's real opportunity."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#3012. New Champion Kimi Antonelli and Lando Norris Trade Blows in Sao Paulo GP Friday Practice

The newly crowned 2026 F1 champion Kimi Antonelli and the outgoing champion Lando Norris traded top spots during Friday practice sessions at the Sao Paulo Grand Prix.

Antonelli, who became the youngest-ever F1 champion at the Mexican Grand Prix last weekend, finished second in FP1 before bouncing back to top the timesheets in FP2. Norris, meanwhile, made an encouraging start to the weekend as he led the opening practice session.

Both the drivers emerged as the leading contenders, showing impressive pace throughout the Friday practice. Red Bull Racing driver Max Verstappen also showed encouraging speed at Interlagos.

Lando Norris led FP1 in Brazil. (Source: MEGA)

The McLaren driver began the Sao Paulo Grand Prix weekend by setting the benchmark in FP1, recording a fastest lap of 1:11.717, ahead of Antonelli.

The Mercedes driver finished 0.082 seconds behind Norris. Verstappen was the third-fastest in the opening session.

The seven-time world champion Lewis Hamilton was fourth for Ferrari with a 1:12.041, while Mercedes' George Russell finished fifth with a lap time of 1:12.103.

FP1 also featured several rookie appearances, as six regular drivers stepped aside for their teams to fulfil the mandatory young-driver practice requirement.

Out of the six test drivers, Gabriele Mini was the fastest as he finished seventh ahead of McLaren's Leonardo Fornaroli in 11th.

Antonelli initially established himself at the front on medium tires before improving his time. However, he struggled slightly during his first soft-tire attempt, locking up and running wide at Turn 1.

Norris, on soft tyres, took the opportunity to move ahead and stayed at the top until the chequered flag.

Kimi Antonelli edged out Lando Norris in FP2. (Source: MEGA)

Antonelli bounced back strongly in the second practice session for Mercedes, setting the fastest lap of the day with a 1:10.763 lap on the soft tyres, ahead of Norris.

The 20-year-old finished 0.253 seconds ahead of Norris, who recorded a 1:11.016. Verstappen came third again in the day, just 0.006 seconds behind the McLaren driver.

Russell briefly moved to second with a 1:11.036 before Norris and Verstappen improved their times. McLaren's Oscar Piastri finished fifth with a lap time of 1:11.084 seconds.

Ferrari drivers endured a quiet start to the Brazil weekend. Hamilton took sixth after struggling to get tire temperatures up in the cooler track conditions. Charles Leclerc finished seventh after losing time when he had to veer toward the wall to avoid Haas debutant Rafael Câmara.

Having already achieved his championship goal, Kimi Antonelli will now look to finish the season strongly and help his team secure the Constructors' Championship.

Mercedes has an opportunity to clinch the teams' title at Interlagos, provided it outscores Ferrari by at least three points over the weekend.

The day 2 action at Interlagos resumes with the final practice session at 11:30 AM local time, followed by qualifying at 3 PM local time.

#3008. Montana votes to ban corporate political money. Next stop? Probably the Supreme Court

Imagine a corporation wants to spend $10 million to defeat a political candidate. Under the Supreme Court's landmark 2010 Citizens United ruling, it can spend as much as it wants provided it doesn't coordinate with the candidate's campaign.

On Tuesday, Montana voted to change that.

In a state President Donald Trump won by nearly 20 points in 2024, roughly 65% of Montanans voted to approve Initiative 194 — the "Montana Plan" — embracing a novel legal strategy to curb corporate political spending. If states cannot restrict political speech under Citizens United, can they instead strip corporations of the power to spend money on elections?

The vote follows a record $11.87 billion in political advertising nationwide this election cycle that was split about evenly between the two parties, according to media tracking firm AdImpact.

The voter-approved measure is set to become state law on Jan. 1 in Montana, where voters can make law via ballot initiatives. Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could amend or repeal it, though proponents have pledged that if that happens they will take the fight back to voters in 2028.

Now, Montana is poised for a constitutional showdown that could reach the nation's highest court. If the strategy survives, it could provide a replicable blueprint for restricting corporate political spending across the country. Similar measures are being explored in more than 30 states.

"If a single state can successfully deny corporate power over elections, it proves that Citizens United wasn't the final word on democracy, and we will continue battling," said Tom Moore, a former Federal Election Commission official and current senior fellow at the Center for American Progress who helped develop the Montana Plan.

The Montana Plan hinges on a legal distinction: Corporations may have constitutional rights, but their powers come from the states.

Under Initiative 194, Montana would strip corporations, trade associations, labor unions and nonprofits, including those headquartered elsewhere, of the legal power to spend money influencing elections in the state.

In practice, that should mean no corporate-funded attack ads, no trade associations bankrolling referendum campaigns and no politically active nonprofits spending their own funds to sway Montana voters.

Violators risk losing state-granted corporate privileges such as limited liability, which generally protects shareholders from personal responsibility for corporate debts, until all improperly spent funds are surrendered and legal conditions are met.

"The argument of the people supporting the initiative is, in theory, trying to be pretty simple. Keep your campaign cash out of Montana, or you run the risk of putting your shareholders' personal balance sheets on the line," said Kal Munis, a politics professor at Montana State University who is an expert in the state's politics.

The financial stakes extend well beyond Montana. Known corporate contributions to outside groups in federal elections surged from $13 million in 2010 to more than $600 million by September 2026, according to OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks such spending. That excludes money funneled through nonprofits that conceal their donors.

But there's an irony to the Montana Plan. It had high-dollar backers from corporate America.

State campaign finance data shows Samantha Walton, wife of Walmart heir Lukas Walton, gave $722,300 to groups backing the plan, while LinkedIn co-founder and Democratic megadonor Reid Hoffman contributed $150,000. The campaign's organizing nonprofit, Transparent Election Initiative, is itself a type of dark-money group that generally isn't required to disclose its donors.

And the new law wouldn't exactly take billionaires out of politics. It exempts individuals, political committees, government entities and bona fide news, commentary and editorial content.

Consider Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, the 2026 election's largest known individual donor, with at least $474 million in political giving, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records. Musk could still spend millions of his personal fortune influencing Montana elections. His companies, however, would face the new restrictions.

Moore argued that because corporations derive their legal existence from states, those states should have the power to restrict their political spending.

Opponents call the Montana Plan an unconstitutional end run around Citizens United. If states cannot directly prohibit protected political speech, they argue, they cannot accomplish the same thing by threatening companies' legal privileges.

And critics warn the restrictions would reach beyond corporate giants, silencing local trade groups, labor unions and community organizations on school bonds, tax measures and other local ballot issues.

"Why allow a media corporation to speak out, while denying a non-profit association of citizens the right to organize around clean water or lower taxes?" said Brad Smith, who leads the Institute for Free Speech and is a former FEC chairman. "Billionaires will find loopholes. All this really does is silence Montana's small- and medium-sized businesses."

The first legal test is already underway in Hawaii, where lawmakers enacted a similar law in May. Montana is the first state to embrace the strategy through a statewide vote.

The Hawaii law faces formidable legal opposition. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce has sued to block it, and the Institute for Free Speech is representing the Grassroot Institute of Hawaii in a separate challenge. The national ACLU has also weighed in, arguing in a court brief that the law violates the First Amendment.

Legal and campaign finance experts told CNBC the dispute could eventually reach the Supreme Court, whose 6-3 conservative majority has repeatedly loosened campaign finance restrictions. Just in June, the justices struck down limits on political parties' spending in coordination with candidates.

"Shifting the prohibition into the corporate code is a distinction without a difference," said Michael Kang, a Northwestern Pritzker School of Law professor who studies campaign finance. "The courts will see right through it because the clear intent remains to bypass Citizens United. It seems like a legal sleight of hand."

Montana has lost this fight before. In 1912, voters banned corporate campaign spending after copper barons bribed lawmakers and dominated state politics. The law stood for a century before the Supreme Court struck it down in 2012 under Citizens United.

"The way we look at it, this is states' rights vs. Citizens United," said Moore, of the Center for American Progress. "That's the pitch once and if it makes it to the Supreme Court they're going to need to decide between one of their two favorite legal theories and things to fall back on."

#3004. A long-term Elon Musk backer is cashing out of Tesla — and cutting its SpaceX exposure

A key early investor has sold the last of its Tesla shares and sharply reduced its SpaceX holding, taking profits from two of its most successful long-term bets.

Scottish Mortgage Investment Trust, a London-listed closed-end fund managed by Baillie Gifford, said Friday that SpaceX represented 11.1% of its total assets as of Wednesday, down from 15% at Sept. 30.

The position helped drive Scottish Mortgage's 31.5% net asset value return in the six months to the end of September. In its half-year results statement, Scottish Mortgage also said it sold its remaining Tesla stake in April, ending an investment that dated back to 2013.

Scottish Mortgage first invested in SpaceX in December 2018, when Elon Musk's rocket company had a reported valuation of about $31 billion, and committed roughly $200 million through 2021.

The investment surged in value as SpaceX expanded its launch business and Starlink satellite network. By the time of its blockbuster IPO in June, SpaceX represented roughly a quarter of Scottish Mortgage's portfolio by value.

The investment trust has since sold shares as post-IPO lock-up restrictions have lifted in stages, though it said its conviction in SpaceX remains high.

"The decision to reduce the position following its flotation, while retaining a substantial investment, demonstrates the importance of balancing conviction with disciplined portfolio management," Scottish Mortgage said.

SpaceX was a major contributor to Scottish Mortgage's performance during the period, adding 8 percentage points to returns.

The trust's net asset value, or NAV, delivered a total return of 31.5% in the six months through Sept. 30, compared with a 16% return for the FTSE All-World Index. Its share price returned 33.8%.

Proceeds from the SpaceX sales also helped Scottish Mortgage repay borrowings and reduce gearing.

The Tesla sale closes another highly profitable bet for Scottish Mortgage, which invests globally in a mix of public and private high-growth companies, typically with a longer-term investment view.

In a portfolio update in July, the fund said it had invested around $470 million in Tesla, and generated approximately $6.2 billion in realized profit over 13 years.

"Since we first invested in 2013, it has returned 13 times the capital invested, but we felt the share price had come to reflect much of its promise in autonomy and robotics," Scottish Mortgage said on Friday.

The trust also pointed to intensifying competition in Tesla's core automotive business, particularly in China.

Shares in SpaceX and Tesla fell about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday, taking their combined market value more than $420 billion below Tuesday’s close. Investors fear Elon Musk could face new congressional probes after the U.S. midterms, potentially exposing his companies to tighter regulation.

Scottish Mortgage is one of several U.K. investment trusts — comparable to closed-end funds in the U.S. — that were early backers of SpaceX.

Another Baillie Gifford fund, Edinburgh Worldwide Investment Trust, was the focus of a bitter activist campaign by Boaz Weinstein's hedge fund Saba Capital in 2025, with its SpaceX holding becoming a key flashpoint in the long-running battle.

#3002. Donovan Mitchell needs to shoot more, not less

If you've been online recently, you may have noticed that Donovan Mitchell has… pissed off a lot of Cleveland Cavaliers fans.

Twitter is ablaze with criticism. People want him traded. Some regret that he was ever traded for. Mitchell has become the crux of all problems; the shot-chucking 'dictator' who will never be the primary option on a championship team.

But everyone has it backwards; Donovan Mitchell needs to shoot more.

Mitchell's field goal attempts and usage rate are at career lows to start the year.

His assist percentage and shot chart are nearly identical to previous seasons.

That means Mitchell has less control over the offense than ever, despite some insisting that he's grown into a ball-dominant tyrant.

How did we get here? And why are fans feeling such a disconnect?

Mitchell has always been a scorer more than a passer. He currently ranks in the 41st percentile in assist-to-usage ratio — a stat that tracks how often a player is ending their possessions with an assist rather than a shot attempt or turnover.

This means that roughly half of the league's players are more likely to get an assist on any given possession than Donovan Mitchell. That can lead to games where it feels like Mitchell is hogging the ball while leaving his teammates hanging.

That gung-ho play style caused some frustration throughout last year's playoffs, and the complaints have carried into this year.

Fans want to know why Evan Mobley and Jarrett Allen aren't getting the ball. Why do their leads disappear so often? And why haven't the Cavs gotten over the hump despite a roaring regular season?

Some have identified Mitchell's lack of playmaking as the problem.

Which, in fairness, is certainly a factor. Cleveland has lost big games in the past due to Mitchell's shot selection. He doesn't always make the right decision with the ball in his hands — and that can be difficult to watch as a fan.

But we should reframe this discussion. Let's start here.

Donovan Mitchell isn't a point guard. And maybe, he doesn't have to be.

Shai-Gilgeous Alexander was in the 29th percentile for assist-to-usage ratio when the Oklahoma City Thunder won the championship. He's in the 0th percentile now.

The truth is, championship-caliber players are allowed to have weak points. You are not disqualified if you can't do everything on your own.

Giannis Antetokounmpo never learned how to shoot. Nikola Jokic never became an elite defender. And the New York Knicks didn't win a championship last June because Jalen Brunson suddenly became an elite playmaker — they won because they delegated more playmaking responsibilities to Karl-Anthony Towns — leaving space for Brunson to do what Brunson does best… score the ball.

All of those teams identified their star's weakness, and built a roster around them that could insulate them.

The Cavs need a similar solution. They need someone who can take playmaking off of Mitchell's plate.

Darius Garland was the original fit. And, for a while, he would thread the needle beautifully. He sent the defense scattering into motion while Mitchell would catch the ball against a defense already on their back feet. That dynamic led to 64 wins — but unfortunately, Garland couldn't stay healthy.

James Harden was the next option. He's been more available than Garland, which was enough to help bolster the team to an Eastern Conference Finals run.

But Harden's style differs from Garland's. He's slower-paced, favoring a heliocentric style that inhibits stationary spacing around him. The result is fewer chances for Mitchell to attack in motion, nerfing his greatest strength as a slasher who can attack gaps.

All of this has culminated in the Cavs (and fans online) asking Mitchell to be more of a playmaker. But that is a fundamental misunderstanding of who he is — and how you win in the NBA by playing to your strengths.

I want to be clear that I am not advocating for Mitchell to go full Dion Waiters and ignore the other four Cavaliers sharing the court with him at any given time. He still needs to pass the ball — and he needs to choose the right moments to assert himself as a scorer.

You can't win by playing like a bull in a China shop. There has to be a delicate balance between scoring in bunches and releasing control of the offense to someone else. That's the key here.

Mitchell will never be a point guard. Any system that asks him to make reads at that level will learn the hard way. You can't force a square peg into a round hole.

You can, however, build a competitive team that understands who Mitchell is.

We know for a fact that Mitchell can go nuclear as a scorer in the playoffs. We've seen it multiple times.

But who can grab the reins and facilitate those high-scoring performances? Who is the Karl-Anthony Towns to his Jalen Brunson in this scenario?

The Cavs are still hoping that will be Harden. Additional support from Mobley would be welcomed. Whether that will be enough is certainly worth questioning.

But here's another cold, hard truth: Making it to the top of the mountain is hard. If we're being honest, it's damn near impossible. This franchise had the greatest player alive for 11 seasons and only made it to the finish line once.

Cleveland would need a myriad of miracles to win a championship with this group. Even if everything went right, they could still fall short. That's just the way it goes.

That's why sticking true to your identity is more important than anything. Being a bad version of someone else will always be inferior to being the best version of yourself.

I don't think Mitchell will lead Cleveland to a title through his playmaking. That feels like jumping the shark.

The Cavs do need him to be better defensively and to make better reads offensively, but that doesn't necessarily mean relinquishing his role as one of the highest-volume scorers in the league. That's who he is — that's who the team traded for.

Mitchell needs to be a world-class scorer. Nearly all of his attention on offense should be centered on getting his shot. Passing will always come second. Winning or losing comes down to Mitchell taking and making the big shots. That was the primary difference between him and Brunson last season.

Of course, any team would prefer a superstar who is capable of blending elite scoring with masterful playmaking and impactful defense. But, you work with what you have — and the Cavs have a dynamic scorer, not a floor general.

Accepting Mitchell's true nature and having the supporting cast insulate him with their playmaking is the most realistic path forward. It's the only path forward.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3001. Raiders Week 9: 49ers explosive offense, stingy defense are imposing

At 6-1 overall, it's no surprise the San Francisco 49ers boast a Top 10 offense and defense at this particular juncture of the 2026 season.

The Niners have the fourth-best scoring offense of the 32 NFL teams at 197 points per game and accumulated the sixth-highest total yards at 2,600. In addition, the team's defense allows the sixth-least amount of points at 139 and the fourth-least amount of yards at 2,095.

The Las Vegas Raiders' Klint Kubiak and Rob Leonard will certainly have their hands full when the Silver & Black head to Santa Clara for a Week 9 matchup with the 49ers. The head coach and defensive coordinator, respectively, are the play callers for offense and defense and have the headset and play sheet to combat their counterparts in San Francisco head coach Kyle Shanahan and defensive coordinator Raheem Morris.

Of the Raiders combo, Leonard has the tougher challenge trying to stymie Shanahan's explosive offense that hasn't skipped a beat even though injuries change the lineup.

"Yeah, they do. They're fundamentally sound, man," Leonard said of the 49ers offense. "They're a clean operation, and they got some good players. Quarterback's playing at a high level, good tight end, running backs, and the offensive line is really good. So, that's what jumps out to me, how fundamentally sound they are, and they're able to put a lot of pressure on you."

With an accurate and collected quarterback in Brock Purdy, Shanahan funnels the offense through his signal caller and it's one that attacks every level of the opposing defense. Very physical in the trenches, the offensive line buys Purdy the time to go through his progressions and get the ball to a target like tight end George Kittle. The 33-year-old veteran leads the team with 34 catches for 425 yards and five touchdowns and shows no signs of slowing down — as evidence by his 56-yard catch-and-run below:

Shanahan's ground game is domineering ranking eighth-best in yards (892) and third in rushing touchdowns (10) and bolsters his ability to open the full play book to deploy on the opponent. Christian McCaffrey paces the run game with 84 carries for 389 yards and eight touchdowns. Spell back Kaelon Black has 57 totes for 233 yards and a score while Purdy has scrambled for 161 yards and a touchdown on 32 attempts.

As fundamentally sound Leonard says the 49ers are on offense, his Raiders defense must be equally as coherent — something Las Vegas has tumbled down the rankings as it lost four-straight games before a Week 8 win against the New York Jets to get to 4-4 overall.

The Silver & Black are giving up the second-most points (230) and third-most yards (3,055). Leonard's unit is ranked 25th against the pass with 1,884 yards allowed and 31st against the run with 1,171 yards yielded. The defensive play caller knows his crew has a lot to work on and can't afford a lapse against a quality team like the Niners. Especially with San Francisco's penchant to run outside zone runs and a multitude of pre-snap motions to create chaos. To combat this, Leonard is likely to use more single-high safety alignments for run fits and creatively blitz to ground the 49ers.

Then — now don't gasp if this brings past pain with the Raiders — maintain eye discipline is vital. The motions Shanahan habitually uses is intended to break coverages that end up explosive plays. Las Vegas can ill-afford to get "caught looking" against San Francisco, a team that's deployed 21 personnel (two running backs and one tight end) to perfection at times this season.

How Leonard counters with personnel is going to be intriguing.

Perhaps going into sub packages built on speed and athleticism that brings slot corner Taron Johnson in the lineup is the route to go. Safeties Treydan Stukes and Jeremy Chinn have shown the ability to stop the run while Johnson is both a steady cover man and tackler over the middle.

"Stack plays," Leonard said when asked what was stressed during practice this week. "Really focusing on us, our alignment, our assignment, key technique, fundamentals, and it's especially that time of the year where it's hard to really out-scheme people. Everybody knows who they are, so you have to go out and execute."

The chess game between the Raiders offense and 49ers defense is the marquee matchup for the Silver & Black. Unlike the defense, Kubiak concocted the sixth-best scoring offense in the league (221 points) that's generated the 12th most yards (2,739). The aerial attack is third-best in the NFL with 2,121 yards and 19 touchdowns.

Morris, meanwhile, is the architect of Niners' sixth-ranked defense in points allowed (139) and fourth-ranked in yards allowed (2,095). Under Morris' command, San Francisco has the fifth-best run defense allowing 623 yards and the second-least rushing touchdowns (three).

With Las Vegas unable to get a consistent ground game going (31st in yards at 618), expect Kubiak to dial up creative passing concepts to get tight end (in-name-only) Brock Bowers loose against a 49ers defense that's 11th-best in yards allowed (1,472) and 17th most touchdowns given up (10).

"It's a talented bunch. Been together for a long time. Experienced bunch," Raiders offensive coordinator Andrew Janocko said of the 49ers defense. "It's a team that have a lot of familiarity with playing against them last year. They're well coached, well prepared. They attack the football. They're very opportunistic. So, it'll be a heck of a challenge for us against one of the best teams in the league."

With how disciplined 49ers defenders play, Las Vegas must win their blocking assignments to keep veteran quarterback Kirk Cousins upright to throw. And to ensure a more productive run game by establishing its own outside zone runs, the offensive line must anchor and push forward and not get stonewalled or driven backwards. A ground game that gains positive yards consistently allows play action to be truly effective — which is a staple of Kubiak's offense.

Sunday's clash marks the 16th time the Raiders and 49ers have met. The Niners lead the all-times series 8-7 (all regular season games) and the Las Vegas variant of the Silver & Black have played the Red & Gold only once: Back on New Year's Day in 2023 which the 49ers won 37-34. San Francisco is riding a two-game win streak with the 2023 meeting the latest one and a 34-3 victory over the then-Oakland Raiders in 2018 being the other. The Raiders last win over the Niners arrived in 2014 — a 24-13 win in Oakland.

"Another top five defense in the league. Feels like I'm saying that every week the last several weeks. I don't know who the bottom five defenses are, but we're not playing them. So, maybe someday. But what a challenge."

—Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Kirk Cousins on the Week 9 matchup

#2999. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2998. AI debt concerns grow as ING reportedly seeks buyers for $500 million of data center loans

ING is reportedly seeking buyers for a portfolio of 12 data center loans worth around $500 million, joining a broader push by banks to distribute credit risk tied to the artificial intelligence infrastructure build-out.

The auction is said to include a $25 million piece linked to the Stargate project in New Mexico, which involves OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle, as well as certain other loan assets involving Vantage Data Centers and CyrusOne, Bloomberg reported Friday, citing people familiar with the matter.

ING will hold a bids-wanted-in-competition, or BWIC, auction process, with bids sized at $10 million or more due by Nov. 19, according to Bloomberg. A BWIC is a competitive process for investors to bid on pieces of a loan portfolio.

The sale highlights a growing market for banks to move data center loans to private credit firms, asset managers and insurers as financing for the AI boom expands.

Investment banks typically use a lend-to-distribute model for these types of loans, said Hank Calenti, global markets strategist at SMBC.

"The buyers can range from private credit investors through asset managers to insurers. They would buy these assets for their expected higher returns," he said.

The ING sale comes as fears grow over soaring debt tied to the AI build-out — and potentially disappointing returns among investors.

Those concerns have accelerated following the Democrats' sweeping win in the U.S. midterms, which could see moratoriums or tighter restrictions on data center construction in certain states.

The perceived risk of a construction slowdown sent AI-related stocks into free fall on Thursday, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closing the session down 5.5% — its worst day since April 2025 — and into correction territory.

"Whether banks sell more based on AI data centers moratorium fears should be driven by the cost (discount received) versus the benefit of exiting to free up cash for further lending, where the cost includes explaining the exposure to your board," Calenti told CNBC via email.

"This could be business as usual, or financiers looking to exit before the door shuts."

Pruksa Iamthongthong, head of APAC equities at Aberdeen Investments, said the Democrats' gains have increased concerns around greater scrutiny of data center development, at a time when investors were already questioning the financing and returns behind the scale of AI capital spending.

"We think near-term volatility could continue as the market assesses potential delays to U.S. data center construction and constraints around compute capacity."

Earlier this year, Wall Street lenders including JPMorgan Chase and Morgan Stanley were said to be exploring a variety of approaches to reduce their lending exposures to data center construction.

According to a Financial Times report on May 3, these include offloading portions of debt via private sales to non-bank lenders, as well as through so-called significant risk transfers, which transfer part of a bank's credit risk to investors to free up balance sheet capacity.

#2994. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2991. Market sell-off hits hard: Advisors say 'stay the course with an eye on buying'

Top financial advisors aren't urging clients to change their portfolios after Thursday's sharp market sell-off, even as retail investors were heavy sellers of individual stocks.

In a Thursday flash poll of advisors from CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council, more than three-quarters of respondents said investors' first steps should be to check their allocation against their goals or stay the course. The online poll, conducted from 2:30 p.m. to 6 p.m. ET on Thursday, was sent to 121 firms and 38 responded.

Retail investors sold individual stocks Thursday as technology stocks tumbled, and the Dow gave back its post-midterm election gains. The S&P 500 suffered its worst one-day drop since April 2025 on Thursday. Analysts linked the sell-off to fears of a data center construction slowdown following major Democratic election wins.

The Nasdaq Composite tumbled 5.5%, closing more than 10% below its most recent high and putting it in what Wall Street regards as a correction. The broad-market S&P 500 lost 4.4%, while the Dow fell more than 1,800 points, or 3.4%.

Yet, financial advisors who answered the CNBC poll overwhelmingly responded that they are not recommending changes to their clients' portfolios. The broader message from interviews with several advisors: review whether your portfolio still matches your goals, but don't let a sudden market move make the decision for you.

For investors who continue contributing to a 401(k), a market decline can mean buying investments at lower prices over time, said Andy Pratt, director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia.

"If you have a 401(k), money is being allocated there biweekly, monthly, you know, whatever your pay period is, and so you're able to dollar-cost average in," Pratt said. "So when the market does sell off, you're entering at a much better spot."

Pratt said a well-designed portfolio may not need changes during a downturn. John Mullen, managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management in Providence, Rhode Island, put it this way: "Stay the course with an eye on buying."

Yet, if investors are panicking, that could be a sign to reassess whether their investments still fit their goals and risk tolerance, financial advisors say.

Carolyn McClanahan, a certified financial planner, physician and founder of Life Planning Partners in Jacksonville, Florida, says she tells clients to follow this strategy, which "works beautifully."

McClanahan said investors should stick to an investment policy that reflects their needs and "tune out the rest of the noise."

Tra Sullivan, a certified financial planner, wealth manager, and chief investment officer at Dallas-based Beaird Harris Wealth Management, said historically the U.S. stock market has often risen in the 12 months after midterm elections, but he views that pattern as context rather than a promise.

He added that when markets fall, "there's always a recovery, and a lot of times, in most cases, more quickly than we anticipate", although past rebounds don't guarantee future recoveries.

Nasdaq futures rebounded in pre-market trading on Friday with a bounce in chip stocks following a sell-off that sent the Nasdaq into correction territory the day before.

Still, if market weakness continues, Sullivan said he brings the discussion back to a client's financial plan.

For long-term investors, Dan Moisand, a CFP and financial advisor at Orlando, Florida-based Moisand Fitzgerald Tamayo, said resilience can matter more than trying to time each market swing.

"Being resilient has proven to be more profitable than trying to be nimble," he said.

Correction: An earlier version of this article misstated John Mullen's certifications. He is managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management.

#2990. Fake job interview scams like the one that hit Binance are getting more sophisticated. Here are the new signs to watch for

Job search scams can cost applicants hundreds or thousands of dollars. But one fake job interview recently impacted over $6 billion in assets.

Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, announced on Tuesday it recently experienced a security breach impacting $6.34 billion in digital assets. The hack was enabled in part by a job seeker's nightmare: bogus job interviews.

While the Binance hackers had their sights set on a much bigger target than an individual job seeker, job-related scams are on the rise. The FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) tallied employment-related losses amounting to roughly $363 million in 2025, up sharply from $264 million the year prior, and $70 million in 2023. The actual figure is likely higher, since many victims of such crimes do not report them.

Those orchestrating fake job scams have many potential targets today "because the job search is taking so long for so many right now," says Stefanie Fackrell, a former recruiter at Google and Nvidia. While October's jobs report showed promise as the U.S. economy added 165,000 jobs, far more than expected, many applicants have been struggling through a low-hire, low-fire market for months.

Artificial intelligence can also make scams more widespread and harder to detect.

"Cyber scams in general are becoming much more sophisticated just because they're evolving at the same speed as AI and public access to AI," says Garber. "AI is going to make things seem more and more real."

In 2025, victims reported losses of almost $13 million to AI-involved employment scams, according to the IC3.

Binance's announcement included an abbreviated play-by-play of how its hack went down. Fackrell and Garber broke down some of the warning signs in how the events unfolded, including what job seekers can learn from the incident.

What happened: A software engineer, who was not named, was contacted through "a legitimate job-hunting platform" by someone posing as a project director, Binance said in its announcement. That person arranged for the engineer to have an initial interview with a purported HR representative and a second-round interview with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

Experts' red flags: A headshot that's clearly AI-generated or otherwise looks uncanny, as well as sparse profile information or activity, and few connections should give you pause, Fackrell says. On LinkedIn, for example, you can click the three dots on a person's profile to open an "About this member" pop-up, where you can see when they joined LinkedIn and if they've verified their identity, workplace and school.

Though some small startups may arrange for an interview with a C-suite executive, it's generally unusual, particularly for a second-round interview, unless you'd be reporting to that person, she adds.

What happened: In the first round, the fake HR representative had a live video interview with the software engineer, even sharing with him a social media account with personal photos and videos to build trust, Binance said. The tactic was "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen social engineering approaches," Binance added in its announcement.

Experts' red flags: A recruiter might bring up commonalities with a candidate like the same alma mater or ex-employer to "bring that human element into the conversation," Fackrell says. But sharing personal social media in a first-round interview is strange, she adds.

In video interviews in general, some red flags might include the person moving unusually slowly or not responding to one of your questions, or their visual skipping a beat, Garber says.

What happened: The second-round interview with the purported CEO of the fake company was conducted on "a popular social chat app," Binance said, and the software engineer was instructed to download "an 'assignment' containing a malicious code repository onto his work computer."

Experts' red flags: Interviews commonly happen over Microsoft Teams, Zoom or Google Meet, not a social chat app. "I don't know anybody that's FaceTiming or WhatsApping any of their candidates," Fackrell says.

While take-home assignments, particularly for technical roles, may use third-party systems, don't download software if you're not familiar with it, Fackrell advises. These assignments should be shared via email through a legitimate system, not over the phone. Job seekers should take interviews on a personal device, not a work computer.

Job sites like LinkedIn and Indeed, and entities like the Federal Trade Commission and Better Business Bureau, also have more information about common scams.

You should not have to provide sensitive information or payment in order to get a job, according to the FTC. Legitimate recruiters will reach out from their corporate email addresses, not a personal address. Other red flags include pressure to move quickly through the hiring process or seeing multiple typos in communications.

"Do your research before clicking, responding or engaging with anybody," Fackrell says.

Take a minute to think about how you were contacted and what you're being asked to do to proceed, Garber suggests, and cross-check job postings against the company's official careers page.

"If seems too good to be true, it definitely is," says Garber.

#2989. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2988. Warriors star reveals eye-opening ‘short-man complex’ admission, fueling rage on court

Just as Draymond Green was coming around on Dillon Brooks, the yearslong feud between two of the NBA's most controversial players was reignited this week, when Brooks came to defend his teammate and Green, seething, grabbed him by the collar of his jersey.

Green was ejected from the Warriors' 116-113 win over the Suns on Monday night. Brooks said immediately following the game that he was trying to "de-escalate" the situation, which began when Green wrapped both arms around Khaman Maluach and threw him to the floor.

Addressing the situation for the first time with reporters after Golden State's 120-100 win over the Heat on Thursday night, Green called out Brooks' point of view on their latest altercation.

"He was talking. He came in talking. Putting his chest on my shoulder. That ain't how you de-escalate," Green said. "So I just wish he stood on the type of business he was on, instead of running to y'all. Like, 'I was trying to de-escalate!' No you wasn't, bro."

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green (23) and Phoenix Suns' Jalen Bridges (15) get into an altercation on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in San Francisco. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green, left, throws down Phoenix Suns' Khaman Maluach in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

Both players like to play the villain, with their rivalry going back to Brooks' time with Memphis. A year after their antics gained national attention during the heated 2022 Western Conference semifinals, Brooks took it to the next level, telling ESPN, "I don't like Draymond at all."

Green this week, however, said that he "had started to turn a corner" on his view of Brooks.

"Ah, man, he may be who he say he is," Green said. "But he not."

Brooks' actions and response to the altercation only confirmed Green's initial belief — that Brooks' tough-guy persona only exists so long as the cameras and microphones are on.

"The words you said on the side of me and walking up to me like that," Green said. "When you're de-escalating you usually go to your teammate. But your teammate's there [on the ground]. So you're not de-escalating anything.

"By the way, there's nothing going on. He's there, I'm standing there. There's nothing really to de-escalate."

It depends on your point of view. The scene underneath the basket was reasonably docile until Brooks ran toward Green and shoved him in the back. Green responded by turning around and grabbing Brooks by the jersey near the neck. But Brooks' teammate had already been thrown down by Green.

The sequence earned Green two technical fouls, resulting in his ejection. The Warriors, who were trailing by 11 at the time, said it helped fire them up on their way to a comeback win.

But Green gave an even more telling look into his worldview with his explanation.

"You've heard of people having short man complex? What's it called–Napoleon? I got a little bit of that when I'm playing basketball," said Green, listed at 6-foot-6 and 235 pounds, "because I'm always smaller than the guys I'm going up against."

Golden State Warriors forward Draymond Green argues a call during the first half of an NBA basketball game against the Miami Heat, Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. AP Photo/Tony Avelar

Despite his size disadvantage, at age 36, Green has more or less played shutdown defense while primarily playing the five this season, a result of Yaxel Lendeborg's emergence at the four.

Matched up against Green Thursday night, Giannis Antetokounmpo (6-11, 250) struggled to steamroll into the paint and finished 4-of-9 from the field. When matched up against anyone else, Antetokounmpo converted 3 of 4 attempts from the field and got to the foul line twice.

Green has similarly more than held his own against Rudy Gobert (7-1, 258), Walker Kessler (7-2, 245) and Nikola Jokić (6-11, 284). He's got to find an edge somehow.

"I'm usually outweighed by anywhere from 20 to 60 pounds. I'm usually outsized from a height perspective, anywhere from 5 to Wemby," Green said, eliciting laughs. "With that, In order to do what I do, you have to have that certain nastiness to you. …

Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors helps up Stephen Curry during a game against the Miami Heat at Chase Center in San Francisco on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"I have to have that tenacity. There's certain things you cannot allow to happen because if you allow it to happen the next game you play, it's gonna happen again. Somebody's gonna try it again."

And, so, that's why Green felt compelled to do more than just prevent Maluach (7-2, 250) from converting the entry pass he received in the paint. He had to send a message.

And, like the Warriors' hopes that night, the rivalry was rekindled, just like that.

#2986. Chiefs Market Movers: Who’s Trending at Midseason?

Week 9 marks the halfway point of the 2026 NFL regular season. The Kansas City Chiefs will take on the New York Jets, but before we get to that game, let's look at who's trending up (the Bulls) and down (the Bears) for the Chiefs through eight weeks:

Running back Emmett Johnson: The absolute star of the loss to the Denver Broncos, Emmett introduced himself to the NFL as a complementary back and as a kick returner. He's elusive, explosive, and high energy. He can catch the football and break big plays. From here forward, he should be sharing touches with Ken Walker (maybe put them both on the field at the same time)— and could be that dude at kick returns if they let him.

Wide receiver Rashee Rice: Rashee needs some good news this week. I'm sure that making this list will make up for what he's got going on. Anyway, I do think the arrow is pointing back up for the current #1 wide receiver on the roster. The Broncos defended him pretty well (in both games), but this is still a 1,000-yard-type guy if he's ever on the field for a full season again. Patrick Mahomes needs a reliable target who can get YAC—Rice is still perfect for that role, and he's likely as hungry as he's ever been. I wouldn't be shocked if Rice has over 100 yds this week and/or next.

Edge rusher R Mason Thomas: I am going to keep buying R Mason Thomas stock, because you just get the feeling it's about to take off. The rookie leads the team in pressure rate and is second in sacks (yes, with one…). His snap share has declined the last two games— and the pass rush struggled in both (losses). RMT might be a guy who gets QB sacks in bunches, and the team needs him. Watch to see if he's back to a bigger role this weekend.

Kicker Harrison Butker: It's hard to pinpoint why a historically great (and clutch) kicker is suddenly costing his team every week. But it's easy to see that it's happening. Butker has missed more kicks than any other active kicker—and he's been paid more than most. His contract makes it tough to cut him, but bringing in other kickers (however controversial) might lead to a change at some point.

Offensive line coach Andy Heck: Along with head coach Andy Reid, the other Andy bears some responsibility for the struggles up front. Not only have they struggled to build enough depth to get through a season, but they also have fielded a group that gets penalized far more than is reasonable. On top of that, there are these mysterious issues that have led to a benching and a winding path to the Reserve/Injured list.

Not to mention the best center in the NFL having a bad snap or two nearly every game. Oh, and apparently, protection was also to blame for the short-yardage failures and even the missed field goal (according to Andy). The big uglies continue to be a problem that seems beyond the Chiefs' ability to solve— even after it has cost them dearly in multiple Super Bowls.

Jacob De Jesus: Chiefs Twitter thought it was funny that he was signed in the midst of wide receiver trade talks. He's not the answer at wide receiver, but he could be an elite punt returner if given a chance…

Ben VanSumeren: Given the short-yardage struggles, it's time to get behind a fullback.

Justin Fields: Given the goal-line struggles, it's past time to get Fields behind Trey Smith and Creed as needed for a push.

The Celtics make 14.3 threes a gamem while the Hornets make 13.0 threes a game. The Celtics need to work to get open and move the ball to find the best shots. If the 3's aren't falling, they need to take the ball inside. And they need to defend the perimeter or the Hornets will bury them in threes.

Play Hard 48 Minutes – The Celtics need to start out strong and play hard to build a lead. They need to play hard throughout the game, with no let up. Often teams that hustle and play harder can beat teams that try to coast. The Celtics should not underestimate the Hornets and they need to be aggressive on the boards, on defense, in taking the ball to the hoop, in fighting for loose balls and just play harder overall throughout the game.

X-FactorsOn The Road – The Celtics are facing the distractions of travel and a hostile crowd and they can't allow those distractions to take away from their focus on the game. The Hornets are home with the home crowd behind them and their own familiar court. The Celtics are just beginning a 4 game road trip and are playing 8 of 12 games on the road this month. They are just 1-2 so far on the road and so they need to figure out winning on the road.

Officiating – Officiating is always an x-factor in every game. Every crew officiates differently. Some call it tight, others let them play. The Celtics need to adjust to how the refs are calling the game and not allow bad calls or no calls to take away their focus from playing the game. The Celtics also have to be aware of the new points of emphasis for this season and try to avoid flopping, off‑arm push‑offs, reckless closeouts, delays before free throws, and illegal screens. They have been especially calling flops and push offs and so the Celtics need to avoid doing both.

#2984. Nasdaq drops into correction territory — here’s what typically happens next

The Nasdaq Composite plunged into correction territory on Thursday. History shows more selling can follow in the near term, but a recovery eventually takes hold three months later.

The tech-heavy index slid more than 5% on Thursday, closing more than 11% below its record reached last month. On Wall Street, any decline greater than 10% is a correction. A 20% pullback qualifies as a bear market.

Across 10 previous correction episodes over the last decade, selloffs in 2025, 2022, 2020 and 2018 were followed by further rough selling over the next one month. But the Nasdaq was back to rally mode again after six months for all those occasions except one: 2022.

Overall, the Nasdaq gained an average of 9.41% from the correction bottom over the following three months and 17.03% over six months.

The January 2022 correction was the one that offered a harsher outcome. The Nasdaq lost another 18.3% over the next six months and was down 24.3% a year later.

The catalyst for this correction was Democrats sweeping the House and Senate in Tuesday's midterm elections. The party also made significant gains in Texas, where Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor after campaigning on an immediate moratorium for data center development.

Investors are now weighing whether tighter state-level restrictions could slow data center construction, putting pressure on companies tied to the artificial intelligence trade.

Frank Cappelleri, founder of CappThesis, pointed to past midterm election cycles for guidance and found that points to more volatility in the near term as well.

The chart analyst said the Nasdaq was higher 12 months after the election in all but one of 13 midterm cycles since 1974.

"The caveat is that the path can be a volatile one," he said.

Cappelleri pointed to 2018, when the Nasdaq rallied the day after Democrats won the House. A sharp December sell-off followed, but the index was ultimately about 15% higher a year after the election, he said.

Chip stocks tumbled Thursday, with the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) falling 12% and for its worst day since the Covid-19 breakout of March 2020.

— CNBC's Fred Imbert contributed to the report

#2983. Week 9 NFL picks against the spread, predictions for every game: Bears have momentum going into Sunday night vs. Buccaneers

Chicago Bears hype is about to get very loud.

This NFL season seems wide open. There isn't one great team yet and there are few trustworthy contenders who have emerged. The Bears seemingly saw it the same way when they traded two first-round picks for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons.

And through one game, it seems like a transformative trade. The Bears had a fantastic performance on a national stage, shutting down the Seahawks in a 34-17 win. Chicago's Super Bowl odds took a leap, as did Caleb Williams' MVP odds. In a season dying for a dominant team to emerge, maybe it's Chicago.

The Bears' offense isn't a question. It has scored 30 points in every game Williams has started and finished (it did score just three points against the Vikings and Williams played three-and-a-half quarters, but that says something about Minnesota's defense). If Simmons has as big of an impact on the defense as it appeared on Monday night, Chicago will be looking really good. Nobody paying attention to the NFL hadn't seen how dominant Simmons is, but it was still impressive to see how quickly he fit in and made plays.

Chicago gets another close-up in prime time this week against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers on Sunday night. Surprisingly, the Buccaneers are far better analytically than their 3-5 record would suggest. They're fifth in DVOA, eight spots ahead of the Bears, and eight in net EPA (expected points added) per play. The Bears are strong in net EPA, coming in third.

The Buccaneers will be completely overshadowed in this game. The Bears will be the focus on Sunday night as they've emerged as one of the NFL's most dangerous teams. That's a reason they're a healthy 6.5-point favorite. That's a lot of points, and the Buccaneers might be secretly pretty good. That's why Buccaneers +6.5 is the pick. And if the Bears blow out Tampa Bay in a big prime-time window, they'll take over as the talk of the NFL, if they're not already.

The Bears are 4-0 in games that Caleb Williams started and finished this season.

Here are the rest of the picks against the spread for Week 9 of the NFL season:

The Bengals and Falcons go to Madrid, Spain, for this one. The Falcons are nearly impossible to figure out. They followed up a win over the undefeated 49ers by losing to a Buccaneers team that didn't complete a pass. That sums up Atlanta's season. We should trust Joe Burrow, who is on a heater, though there are more questions about Cincinnati's defense after it gave up 36 points to the Titans at home last week.

Maybe Joe Milton III gives Philadelphia a boost and the Eagles get their first win since Week 2. But since Jalen Hurts went on IR, the Eagles have lost all three games by double digits. It's not a good team in any way, and Milton is far from a sure thing. The Giants are off a bye and should be feeling good coming into this one.

The Broncos are the better team. A few things work against them in this spot: It's an early start on the East Coast, and the Panthers have three days rest advantage. The Broncos are also coming off an emotional win over the Chiefs and there could be a letdown. Denver has been much better at home this season. Carolina has faded after a good start but this is a decent opportunity for a win.

Give Miami credit for playing hard. Maybe at some point that effort starts to fade, but the last time they truly got blown out was Week 2. They lost by 14 points to the Chiefs, but Kansas City led by only seven points with three minutes left. It's possible the Lions score enough to cover, but remember that they're usually a different team outdoors.

Kansas City will enjoy the breather after facing the Seattle and Denver defenses on the road the past two weeks. This seems like the type of game in which the Chiefs could take out their frustrations on an opponent that does have some talent but has won only once since Week 1.

The Browns are likely going with Shedeur Sanders at quarterback because Deshaun Watson was put in concussion protocol. It's not like Watson was playing so well he's irreplaceable, but Sanders has thrown only three passes all season. The Saints are well rested coming off a bye. The Saints are not a great team this season, but this might be a spot for them to get right.

Is it time to trust the Rams? They have covered two games in a row without a sweat. Those wins came against mediocre (Raiders) or bad (Chargers) teams, but they did handle business. And the Commanders are mediocre at best and maybe just bad. Let's see if the Rams are truly back.

It is incredibly hard to trust the Cowboys, especially as a road favorite. But what we saw from the Colts last week in a 42-7 loss was troubling. There was no fight left in that team, which has lost key players to trades and knows its season is done. This could be a bad mistake, trusting a defense that can't stop anyone to cover almost a touchdown on the road. Taking the Colts at this point is even harder.

It's a lot for Houston to be favored by this much on the road, however, home-field advantage has been mostly eliminated this NFL season and the Chargers have no home-field advantage — even if that was still a thing. Houston is coming off a bye and still has hopes to revive its season. The Texans still have a 34.4% chance to make the playoffs according to DVOA. The Chargers are 0.0% on that list. They'll cover a spread at some point but it's hard to anticipate when it happens.

The Raiders have four losses, and they came against four of the better teams in the NFL: Chiefs, Patriots, Bills and Rams. The bad news is this week they get the Super Bowl favorites. The 49ers should be getting many players back after their bye. But the Raiders can move the ball and they're getting a lot of points.

With all the quarterback chaos this week, we get two teams that had a different quarterback in Week 8. Brissett was benched and traded, and Gardner Minshew II takes over. Sam Darnold has a quad injury and he'll be out, with Drew Lock taking over. Lock is a reasonable replacement, and Minshew isn't facing the Cowboys defense this week.

The Patriots have a lot of injuries and the Packers have a few days of extra rest. In a game that seems evenly matched, the small edges add up. Green Bay started the season very slow but has looked much better over the last four games, winning three and barely losing at Detroit. New England has also emerged after an early-season slump to win four of five. This is a really fun matchup, and even enough to feel OK taking the points.

This is an interesting game because the teams have opposite strengths. The Bills have a great offense and bad defense, and the Vikings have a bad offense and great defense. The Minnesota defense held the Bears to three points after all; Chicago has scored 30 points in every other Caleb Williams start. It will be a great test against Josh Allen, who is having another fantastic season. It's never enjoyable to be going against Allen, but getting 3.5 points with a defense this good seems right … as long as the offense does at least a little bit.

#2982. Luna Fluxa Cross Steps Into the Vegas Spotlight

Luna Fluxa Cross is about to take another step in her meteoric sporting career. The 16 year old Spanish driver has been confirmed as a Wild Card for the final round of the F1 Academy in Las Vegas, an opportunity that will allow her to measure herself against the best drivers in the women's category and demonstrate that her talent does not understand ages or barriers. Her name, which was already resonating strongly in the paddock, now becomes a reality on the asphalt.

Fluxa Cross's story is that of a child prodigy who has been burning through stages at vertiginous speed. In 2022, at just 12 years old, she became the first woman to be part of Mercedes' junior program, an endorsement that confirmed the potential that many saw in her since her beginnings in karting. Two years later she was selected as a F1 Academy Discover Your Drive karter for the Champions of the Future Academy Program, a platform that sought female talent in the lower categories but her great leap in quality came with the OK N Senior title: Fluxa Cross achieved six victories and was proclaimed champion with 125 points of advantage over her closest rival, an achievement that made her the first woman to win an FIA international championship since Susanna 'Susy' Raganelli took the Karting World Championship in 1966, a milestone that placed her on the map and that demonstrated that talent has no gender.

Russell, at War With Himself: "I Had Never Fluctuated So Much"The Briton admits that the irregularity of his performance in 2026 is an unprecedented challenge in his career.

This season, the Spaniard has made the jump to single seaters. Fluxa Cross achieved her first podium in the Eurocup 4 Spanish Winter Championship before disputing a full season in the Spanish F4 Championship, furthermore she was selected to participate in the F1 Academy Rookie Test at the Circuit of Navarra where she finished sixth in both sessions. Performances that convinced the category's executives to give her the opportunity to debut in Las Vegas.

"I am incredibly excited to announce that I will be joining the F1 Academy grid as a Wild Card in Las Vegas! It is an incredible opportunity and a very special experience that I am looking forward to living, I can't wait to go out on track and make the most of the weekend" Fluxa Cross declared upon learning of the news, the Spaniard will drive car number 10 of the Hitech team with the sponsorship of Visit Las Vegas, in the sixth and final round of the season.

Toto Wolff Takes Blame for George Russell's StrugglesThe Mercedes director admits that the W17's narrow operating window is weighing down the Briton, who finished eighth in Austin.

Her debut in the F1 Academy is one more step on a path that seems to have no ceiling: Mercedes has placed enormous trust in her, and her progression in the lower categories invites dreaming of a brilliant future in motorsport. The F1 Academy, a category created to boost female talent, finds in Fluxa Cross one of its great ambassadors, her presence in Las Vegas will not only add competitiveness to the grid but will also serve as inspiration for many young women who dream of one day reaching Formula 1.

The event in Las Vegas will be a test of fire for Fluxa Cross, but also a celebration. The Spaniard has worked tirelessly to get here and her debut in the F1 Academy is the recognition of that effort. The future of women's motorsport passes through drivers like her, capable of breaking barriers and demonstrating that talent does not understand genders, Luna Fluxa Cross is ready to shine in Las Vegas and the world of motorsport, attentive, awaits her moment.
