#2981. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2979. Wall Street's fear gauge may be flashing a buy sign

There's nothing like a good selloff to turn a trader into an investor – so the saying goes. It's an adage that echoes the sentiment of an even older one: buy when there's blood in the streets. The maxims ring true after Thursday's selloff in stocks that sent the Cboe Volatility Index (VIX) to its biggest one-day move since April 2025's "Liberation" week for tariffs, according to data from Bespoke Investment Group. In the 19 instances since 1990 in which the VIX rose at least five points from under 20 – it surged more than 11 points from 16.6 to 28.2 Thursday – the median return for the S&P 500 Index three months later was 4.2%, and 4.8% six months later, according to Bespoke analysis that also showed returns were positive 68% and 79% of the time over each period, respectively. "These types of sharp spikes from placid levels are like a line of thunderstorms coming through in summer," said Paul Hickey, co-founder of Bespoke. "The sky quickly darkens and wind gusts, but the loudest thunder and brightest lightning doesn't last long."

To be sure, the data do show the storms can be disruptive near-term. While the median return one month after such events was 2.1%, the average return was negative 0.4%, thanks to a few big downdrafts following volatility surges in 1990 and more recently, Feb. 24, 2020, when the Covid selloff was just getting started. According to X account OddStats, there were only 12 other days in stock market history prior to Thursday when the S&P 500 lost 4% and the Nasdaq 100 Index lost 6% – including one during Liberation week 2025 and three instances during the 2020 pandemic.Tech volatility was even more pronounced as the Nasdaq 100 posted its worst day since March 2020. The Cboe Nasdaq Volatility Index VXN jumped more than 13 points to 38.3, the highest since April 11, 2025. The selling was so intense in chipmakers that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) traded below its net asset value. Yet options traders were still leaning bearish in their trades into the bell Thursday, with 670,000 puts traded out of the total 900,000 options on the ETF. Of the total $1.7 billion options premium exchanged, $1.5 billion of it was in puts, according to SpotGamma.

After Porziņģis' contract is used to facilitate a trade — likely sooner rather than later — he can formally announce his retirement.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

#2970. LeBron's return to Cleveland highlights a new reality: Sixers' offense is a work in progress

CLEVELAND — About an hour before tipoff, 76ers forward LeBron James emerged from the visitors' tunnel, with a collection of fans gathered near the southeast corner of Rocket Arena cheering triumphantly. It was impossible to ignore what could have been.

It was like a scene from the TV series Dark Matter, where the protagonist finds himself in an alternate reality and ponders the possibility of playing it out. A world exists, somewhere in the basketball multiverse, where James decides to ride off into the sunset donning the Cavaliers' wine and gold — not the 76ers' red, white and blue.

On Friday night, aside from the very obvious fact that James indeed plays for Philadelphia now, everything else felt normal in Cleveland: the pregame warm-up, rife with greetings and on-court drills, the same ones he had done in this very building for years; the chalk toss in front of the Cavaliers' faithful. Even the in-arena announcer's energy when James was introduced last was met with raucous applause. And when he checked out with four minutes remaining, a standing ovation ensued.

But the most prominent theme from Dark Matter is that no matter how familiar a place looks and feels, it's not your reality. And James was reminded of that.

"Every game has its own challenges," James said following the Sixers' 119-95 loss. "I don't think [we were sharp] offensively as a group and I know I damn sure wasn't good at all. Look at the film and try to get better for Sunday."

It would be somewhat nitpicky to overly dissect the 76ers' performance on Friday after an 8-2 start over their first 10 games — especially considering how much doubt and uncertainty existed about multiple superstars co-existing. But something has to be said about an offense (which ranked 21st in efficiency before tip-off and will continue to drop after mustering only 95 points) that has yet to jell.

In their second meeting with Cleveland, Philly didn't operate overwhelmingly loose with the ball (with 16 total turnovers), but there was no purpose to their ball movement. Most of their half-court actions were stagnant and head coach Nick Nurse's rotations — designed to stagger their superpowers — only led to less flow and control.

Joel Embiid, who finished with just six points on 0-for-9 shooting, continued his floor-spacing woes. He's now shooting an abysmal 3-for-28 from deep, which puts even more pressure on the likes of James, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown to keep defenses honest. The 76ers won't win many games where Embiid is that inefficient and dominates the ball like he did, with a whopping 41.7 usage rate in the opening 12 minutes.

"I believe in myself," Embiid said. "But when you're in these moments and nothing is going in, it's hard to not look at yourself and be down. But you have to keep going and working."

But isn't this why LeBron chose to come here in the first place? It wasn't because the 76ers were necessarily seeking another offensive weapon — they were in desperate need of his offensive mind.

Coming into Friday night's game, James ranked in the 96th percentile in assist rate and his assist-to-usage ratio was at the highest of his career. Conventional logic says maybe give the guy who's among the league leaders in potential assists, rim assist rate and offensive RAPM the ball as much as possible (his current output is 10% lower than his career usage) — as well as keep Maxey on the floor as much as possible (Philly's already woeful offense falls off a cliff when he sits).

Some of their playmaking metrics are noisy, partially because the sample size is still small after only 10 games and partially because of their sheer star power. For instance, of lineups that have played at least 50 minutes together, the 76ers' starters are taking good shots (seventh in shot quality per PBB Stats). They're also eighth in passes made per game and seventh in secondary assists, which indicates a concerted effort to move the ball and generate good looks. This, to me, is more important than ranking 18th in assists and 21st in potential assists, both of which stem from shot-making luck.

Friday was an outlier in that James coughed the ball up six times, but his other offensive struggles — eight points on 12 shots — weren't that much different than what the 41-year-old has endured this season, with the lowest points per shot attempt of a long, successful career.

"Too many turnovers on my behalf," James said. "And I didn't put in a rhythm offensively all game. Couple of them were careless, couple of them were trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

The 76ers have started the season on a high note and, thanks to an impressive, aggressive defense, have silenced a few naysayers in the opening weeks of the new campaign. But until their offense can find a happy medium, one that keeps James at the mental wheel and highlights the other stars in their respective roles — or if he can rediscover his scoring efficiency — their dreams of serious contention could be another alternate reality door.

#2969. Democratic sweep gives markets a reason to rethink path of interest rates

The Democrats' resounding victory in the U.S. midterms has the potential to curb interest rate hikes globally next year, according to market watchers — but the uncertain path of oil prices, U.S. economic growth and fiscal policies will be key.

Central banks around the world are back in monetary tightening mode, with the Federal Reserve, Bank of England, European Central Bank and Bank of Japan all raising rates in recent months.

The shift has in part stemmed from the U.S. and Israel's war with Iran driving up prices in oil and gas markets. The war has also exacerbated a squeeze in global refining capacity which has sent the cost of products like diesel, gasoline and jet fuel soaring.

Traders largely expected big Democratic gains this week after polls pointed in their favor, but there was still uncertainty leading up to the vote about whether the party would capture both chambers of Congress, increasing the ability for lawmakers to challenge President Donald Trump's policymaking.

Crude oil prices tumbled following the clear-cut Democratic victory in both the House and Senate, with international benchmark ICE Brent crude falling 8.7% on Wednesday, closing below $100 a barrel for the first time since September.

That was largely attributed to raised expectations that the result may mean Trump is more inclined to strike a deal to end the war in Iran, helping restore Gulf refinery capacity and improve export conditions. Vice President JD Vance's lengthy statement blaming Republican losses on the Iran war boosted that view.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said there had been "an admission from the Republican camp that the Iran war cost them a clean sweep in the election, which the market has taken to indicate that a swift resolution to the Middle East conflict could help them save face."

"Trump might relish the opportunity to take all the credit if an end to the war leads to a big drop in energy costs."

If the election result "pushes for a resolution of the conflict in the Middle East, that might bring oil down, and that's been an issue for the Fed and inflation, and part of the reason why rates are higher," said Andrew Slimmon, senior portfolio manager and head of the Applied Equity Advisors at Morgan Stanley.

Historic data shows a tight correlation between oil prices and global rates, Ralph Axel, rates strategist at Bank of America, said in a research note published Thursday. Higher oil prices over the next three to six months would likely mean "significantly higher interest rates" in the U.S., but especially so in the U.K. and Germany, he said.

"If oil prices rise, we think rates will too, especially at the front end where long-end term premium is less of a factor and monetary policy expectations play a larger role," he added.

As the uptick in crude prices on Thursday and choppiness on Friday showed, a resolution to the nearly nine-month-long conflict is far from locked in.

"For now, oil has begun to climb back up again, and interest rate expectations have not radically changed from pre-election levels," AJ Bell's Dan Coatsworth noted.

Money markets on Thursday indicated a moderate change in expectations from a week ago, both in the U.S. and Europe, with traders pricing in a slightly higher chance of monetary easing on a one-year horizon.

The effect on a two-year timeframe was more pronounced in the euro area than the U.S., according to CNBC analysis of LSEG data, with rate pricing down 8.7 basis points on the week for the ECB versus 4.6 basis points for the Fed.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage," said Laurel Rapp, director for the U.S. and North America Program at Chatham House. "We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter."

Eurasia Group's Clayton Allen meanwhile told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that while the war with Iran is unpopular at home, leaving the Middle East region without tangible wins would also be unpopular and that he expected an escalation within weeks.

Andrew Bailey, governor of the Bank of England — which hiked rates by 25 basis points on Thursday — told CNBC that energy price pressures stemmed from a combination of obstructions on the Strait of Hormuz, a shortage in refining capacity, and low European gas storage.

"A peace process in the Middle East will not shift all of those elements," he told CNBC's Steve Sedgwick on Thursday.

"Let's wait and see, because we have seen that fall in energy prices over the last 48 hours, but it is a very volatile situation, as we've learnt over the last seven, eight, nine months."

Ken Egan, head of European Sovereign Credit at KBRA, said the interest rate outlook would be primarily impacted by whether economies have enough underlying momentum for existing inflation pressures to become more persistent.

Europe is "heading into winter after months of elevated energy prices, and the full impact is still feeding through to the wider economy," he said by email.

"The concern for central banks is whether these pressures start to push up wages and prices more broadly, or whether weaker demand limits that pass-through."

A key question for the Federal Reserve will be whether the blue sweep "could lead to more fiscal prudence in the U.S. and thus less inflation," Jane Foley, head of FX strategy at Rabobank, said by email.

"It is very early, however, to be sure of the fiscal outlook, not least because many Democrats were voted in to soften cost of living pressures ... so it is very difficult to come to a firm conclusion at this point as to how the outlook for rates will change next year," she said.

The high level of U.S. fiscal stimulus and whether it is about to change is the "million dollar question" right now, James Sullivan, managing director and head of Asia Pacific equity research at J.P. Morgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

"The reasons why you're seeing inflation running high is a combination of a range of factors, from the war in Iran, to tariffs, to significant tightness in particular areas of the energy complex," he said.

"But it's also important to recognize the U.S. government has continued to spend at very high levels, into an economic expansion with low unemployment, and that's driving very specific macroeconomic implications. If that changes, you potentially take the pressure off the rate environment in a way that gives the Fed significantly more forward flexibility."

Daniel Clifton, head of policy research at Baird Strategas, said the impact on the data center buildout in the U.S. was another big factor to watch.

"In 2025, we had major fiscal headwinds, and it really didn't matter because we had such a big capex boom in the AI build-out last year. This year was driven a lot by stimulus," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"If you have an environment where you have tighter fiscal policy — the Fed is raising rates right now — and you have a slowdown in the AI capex build out, you're probably going to reach a policy mistake at some point in mid-2027," he said.

"That's what happened in 2000 during the Y2K tech boom, the Fed was raising rates, we had higher oil prices, and they just went too far, and eventually it had a recession. Our forecast is not for a recession in the United States. Our forecast is that we'll have a mid-cycle slowdown in 2027."

#2968. 49ers-Raiders predictions: All eyes on Nick Bosa and DeForest Buckner

The San Francisco 49ers are coming off their bye week with some added reinforcements, as they look to start the second half of the season on the right track against the Las Vegas Raiders this weekend.

The Niners are 9.5-point favorites on FanDuel Sportsbook, with the game total set at 51.5.

Stay connected to the action and share every reaction with fellow 49ers fans on the SB Nation app.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

While the 49ers will not have wide receivers Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel, or Christian Kirk, Demarcus Robinson is trending toward playing, while cornerbacks Upton Stout and Nate Hobbs are looking in that direction as well.

Additionally, San Francisco will have Nick Bosa back, so he and DeForest Buckner should be on the field on Sunday as the 49ers debut their new-look defensive line.

Heading into the weekend, the 49ers are massive 10-point favorites over the Raiders after opening as 8.5-point favorites, with the total skyrocketing to 51.5 points.

While the 49ers are tied for the league's best record at 6-1, the Raiders have been on a bit of a slide after their hot 3-0 start. They suffered four straight losses to the Kansas City Chiefs, New England Patriots, Buffalo Bills, and Los Angeles Rams before their come-from-behind win over the New York Jets last weekend.

So, let's look at our three predictions for Sunday's game, which is set to kick off at 1:25 p.m. on FOX.

Demarcus Robinson returns with a big play

The 49ers have missed that vertical field-stretcher since Demarcus Robinson went out weeks ago. Initially, that was set to be De'Zhaun Stribling, but he suffered the ankle injury back in Week 1. So, that went to Demarcus Robinson, who played in just a game and a half before suffering a high ankle sprain.

Robinson has been out since Week 2, but he was involved in both of the games he played to start the year. He had the long 39-yard touchdown against the Rams in Week 1, finishing with two catches for 50 yards.

Then, in Week 2 against the Miami Dolphins, he had three catches for 34 yards, with the longest going for 19.

The 49ers wide receiver core is still beaten up, and the group has struggled with separation at times. That's where Robinson comes in, and it feels like Kyle Shanahan will look his way for a couple of vertical plays. I think one hits on Sunday.

Nick Bosa, DeForest Buckner return with a sack

Kirk Cousins has taken 11 sacks this season, as Klint Kubiak and the Raiders have done a great job getting the ball out of his hands. Cousins has averaged a 2.70-second time to throw, and he's been even quicker against the blitz.

All 11 of his sacks have come when teams have rushed four or fewer, and I think the 49ers will find their way to at least one courtesy of returners Nick Bosa and DeForest Buckner this weekend.

Las Vegas's run game has not been great. That should leave the Raiders in some tough late-down situations, and the 49ers suddenly have a pretty impressive veteran pass-rushing group with Bosa, Buckner, and Osa Odighizuwa.

Now, others might get home for even more sacks, but I think the stars are aligning for Bosa or Buckner to get to the quarterback at least once on Sunday.

One way the Raiders' passing game has been thriving this season is by throwing to Ashton Jeanty, one of the most targeted running backs in the NFL this season.

Now, Jeanty may have gotten a soft benching last week, as his snap share dipped significantly in favor of rookie Mike Washington, but he's still a very talented pass catcher, and that leaves Fred Warner with the top assignment this weekend.

Not only will Warner likely carry star Brock Bowers for a few routes, but he'll also work in coverage against the running backs, the two spots Las Vegas has looked to target.

Jeanty is second on the Raiders with 37 receptions for 376 yards and three touchdowns. The 49ers will have to mitigate that on Sunday.

#2966. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2964. Oklahoma State's balanced offense overwhelms Queens in blowout victory

STILLWATER, Okla. — Oklahoma State men's basketball put its offensive depth and unselfish play on full display Friday night, cruising to a 118-68 victory over Queens behind a barrage of three-pointers and contributions throughout the lineup.

The Cowboys (2-0) finished with 30 assists on 44 made field goals, shot 62% from the field and connected on 16 of 28 three-point attempts. Andrija Grbovic led Oklahoma State with 17 points, while Parker Robinson added 14, Julius Halaifonua scored 13 and Latrell Almond finished with 12. Jordan Walker contributed 11 points, and Ryan Crotty added 10.

Queens entered the matchup with a 2-0 record after averaging 95.5 points through its first two games. The Royals also returned four players from last season's Atlantic Sun championship team that reached the NCAA Tournament. However, Oklahoma State quickly established control with its transition offense, perimeter shooting and ball movement.

Halaifonua scored the Cowboys' first two field goals, connecting on a three-pointer before finishing inside. Kashie Natt followed with a putback, and Jordan Burks threw down a transition dunk off a steal to give Oklahoma State a 10-6 advantage.

The Cowboys began separating themselves midway through the first half as Grbovic caught fire from beyond the arc. After connecting on a corner three-pointer, Grbovic added another from the wing before knocking down consecutive triples to extend Oklahoma State's lead to 30-10.

The offensive explosion featured several highlight-reel finishes. Almond threw down a powerful dunk off a flashy pass from Natt, while Anthony Felesi finished another slam following an assist from Kayne Cleary. Walker added a wide-open three-pointer, and Burks later finished a transition dunk off a full-court pass from Luka Bogavac. Oklahoma State carried a 52-31 advantage into halftime, shooting 48.7% from the field and 53.8% from three-point range.

Perhaps the most impressive first-half numbers were the Cowboys' 15 assists on 19 made baskets and 25 points from their bench. Queens, meanwhile, struggled to find its shooting touch, connecting on just 2 of 17 three-point attempts before halftime.

Any hopes of a second-half comeback quickly disappeared. Natt scored on a drive to the basket just 16 seconds into the half. Felesi then connected with Walker for an alley-oop dunk in transition, and Bogavac added a fast-break layup. Almond followed with two dunks, including one off a steal, as Oklahoma State opened the half with 14 unanswered points to extend its advantage to 66-31.

The Cowboys' lead reached 40 points with approximately 14 minutes remaining, allowing coach Steve Lutz to continue utilizing his bench. Oklahoma State surpassed the century mark when Halaifonua connected on a three-pointer with 7:44 remaining, pushing the lead to 101-53. The Cowboys continued scoring throughout the closing minutes before completing the 50-point victory.

Nearly every Oklahoma State player who entered the game scored. Cleary was the lone exception, playing six minutes as he works his way back from an offseason injury under a minutes restriction.

Oklahoma State's final shooting numbers reflected its offensive dominance. The Cowboys shot 62% overall, 57.1% from three-point range and 14-of-19 at the free-throw line. The 30 assists were another indication of the team's offensive chemistry, with Oklahoma State consistently finding open shooters and creating opportunities in transition.

After opening the season with a 91-60 victory over Incarnate Word, the Cowboys have now scored 209 points through their first two games.

While tougher competition awaits, including an upcoming road matchup against Texas A&M, Oklahoma State has demonstrated its scoring potential and depth during an impressive opening week. For Lutz and the Cowboys, Friday's performance provided another encouraging glimpse of what their new-look roster can accomplish when the offense is clicking.

#2963. AI crush is powering one stock to sky-high valuations

The 2026 midterm elections have profoundly altered the national regulatory environment, most notably with Democrats securing control of both chambers of the U.S. Congress and notching big victories in traditionally business-friendly states like Texas and South Carolina.

Over the past two decades, Southern states like Florida and Texas have attracted massive corporate migration from blue states. With less regulation and lower taxes. Those same benefits also attracted many people seeking lower taxes, lower housing costs, and better economic opportunities. With this political shift, relocated companies may now face a landscape increasingly focused on energy and building constraints, and tighter legislative oversight.

Information technology, semiconductors, and traditional energy sectors experienced immediate headwinds following the election. Companies at the forefront of the AI boom, such as CoreWeave, Tesla, and SpaceX, saw share prices slide as markets priced in tighter Democratic regulatory oversight and potential restrictions on AI deployment. Senator Bernie Sanders, for example, reposted his efforts to advance a data center moratorium proposal he co-authored with New York Congresswoman and socialist Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez.

The market is suddenly realizing that, between shifts in Washington and in states like Texas, the fastest-growing area of the U.S. economy might grind to a halt. Over half of the U.S. hyperscale data center pipeline could now face heightened regulatory friction. To build new facilities, even assuming they get permitted at all, developers may face tax increases, strict "bring your own power" mandates, or new air-permit delays for on-site gas plants. As all-in construction costs rise and timelines extend, immediate demand for advanced chips snowballs down the hardware supply chain.

Traditional oil and gas majors sank, while solar and green energy stocks surged, driven by expectations that a Democratic Congress will resume pushing subsidies for them. Also, the ongoing U.S.-Israel war with Iran has driven crude prices sharply higher throughout the year. With Democrats taking control of Congress, President Trump faces a legislative body that can severely constrain his military options and push back on defense budgets. Tehran almost certainly recognized that a politically weakened Trump administration would have a greater incentive to seek a negotiated deal, something Secretary of State Marco Rubio has now indicated is preferable to any escalating kinetic options. Consequently, crude prices plunged, with Brent dropping below $95 per barrel.

For similar reasons, defense contractors took market hits due to anticipated congressional resistance to the $1.5 trillion Pentagon budget. Although healthcare - as measured by XLV at least came in today, it remains above pre-election levels, along with grocery stocks such as Walmart, Costco, Kroger, and Dollar General, on expectations that a Democratic Congress might boost federal spending in those areas.

This may help explain why Costco was one of the relatively few stocks to close higher on Thursday, and why it also ranked as the 15th most active single-stock option (in notional terms), ahead of names like JP Morgan, Marvell, Oracle, Eli Lilly, and Dell.

At roughly 46x forward earnings, Costco (a perennial on the "Holly Index (MELLOY, CAN WE LINK TO THIS") isn't cheap, but then it is never cheap. The average forward multiple is actually a few turns higher at over 49x. So what were folks trading? Mostly short-dated call options. One possible reason? Costco isn't just trading at a relatively high multiple; as usual, it is also simply a high-priced stock in dollar terms, at over $1,065 as of Thursday's close. A "round lot" (100 shares) would therefore cost over $100,000 to initiate. Like many things packaged at Costco, that might be a little more than some buyers need (or want) to spend.

Alternatively, call options let investors gain exposure to 100 shares for far less than buying the shares. Buying the January 1060 calls, for example, would cost about $53.45, or $5,345 for upside participation on 100 shares of stock above the $1,060 strike price. That may seem more palatable, but a keen observer might notice that prior highs occasionally present some technical resistance, and Costco topped out at about $1,090 back in mid-May. By selling a call at or near that level, such as the January 1090s at 39.65, one could reduce the cost of the trade even further, expecting a pause before new highs. This would reduce the cost of the trade to $13.80, or just under 1.3% of the current stock price.

I won't pretend $1,380 is a small outlay for a one-lot call spread on a much-loved stock trading at 46x forward earnings. Nor is $520 for 8 6-ounce A5 Wagyu filet mignons (a Costco online only deal available now). But relatively, both the steaks at Costco and the options on Costco are very fairly priced. But it does provide participation in 100 shares of Costco between the strikes, for only 30% more than the cost of a single share of the underlying stock.

#2962. Brandon Aiyuk keeps motive close to vest in statement outside 49ers office

SANTA CLARA — A large, black Mercedes van pulled up outside the 49ers' team offices, a stone's throw from Levi's Stadium, the side door swung open and out stepped Brandon Aiyuk.

The erstwhile wide receiver ended his longstanding refusal to step foot on the team's property Friday afternoon, appearing just before 3 p.m. for a meeting with front office officials.

Both sides kept their cards close to the vest, but the summit represented the first step in Aiyuk resuming his NFL career that has been on pause since injuring his knee in Week 7 of the 2024 season.

San Francisco 49ers wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk warms up before an NFL football game against the Arizona Cardinals in Santa Clara, Calif., Oct. 6, 2024. AP Photo/Jed Jacobsohn

The 49ers made no acknowledgment of the meeting besides coach Kyle Shanahan, who was asked about it Friday and said he did not plan on being involved in any way.

"It's been over for our football team for a long time," Shanahan said. "So I don't hear much, except questions about him today."

A little more than an hour after they arrived, Aiyuk and Drew Rosenhaus emerged from the office building with "nothing to report," according to the agent, who only began representing Aiyuk this week.

Aiyuk responded to one question from the California Post: Who was in the meeting?

"I met with the bosses, man," he said. "Big bosses. Big business."

There remained no immediate resolution to Aiyuk's situation. Currently going unpaid on the 49ers' reserve/left squad list, the 28-year-old receiver must first apply for reinstatement to the NFL.

In a brief statement upon arriving at the team offices, Aiyuk said that was the goal.

"Nice to see y'all," Aiyuk said, greeting the horde of about a dozen reporters and cameramen, wearing a matching Adidas camouflage hat and track jacket with dark blue pants.

"I'm feeling great, feeling healthy. Long recovery process, long rehab process. But I'm back to 100% and looking to get reinstated into the National Football League."

The stakeout spectacle was put into motion when ESPN insider Adam Schefter broke the news Monday that Aiyuk had hired Rosenhaus, a longtime and respected NFL agent, with the intent to get back to football.

The drama kicked into high gear Friday morning, when Schefter reported that Aiyuk and his new agent were en route to the 49ers' offices for a 3 p.m. meeting with team officials.

Shanahan's weekly Friday news conference that typically lasts 10-15 minutes was over in three minutes as reporters rushed to get eyes on the receiver who has been persona non grata around these parts for the better part of the past year and a half.

A television news chopper hovered overhead, tracking the van carrying Aiyuk and Rosenhaus. At one point, the van pulled up to the 49ers' team offices about an hour prior to the meeting, spotted four reporters already staking out the facility and turned around.

An hour later, the same van reappeared, and Aiyuk and Rosenhaus emerged.

"Obviously Brandon is here to meet with the team. Fortunately he's feeling good physically. He's healthy. He's ready to get back to work," Rosenhaus said in a prepared statement. "Don't really have any comment on where he's going to play. We'll keep that between us and the Niners. But obviously we're here with all the right intentions."

It will take more than the right intentions for Aiyuk to resume his NFL career, even if it's a step in the right direction. Despite signing a $120 million extension before the 2024 season, Aiyuk hasn't showed up to the team facilities since last fall and hasn't appeared in a game since that October.

Shanahan has repeatedly dismissed the idea of Aiyuk ever playing another snap for the 49ers, and again Friday afternoon, about two hours before Aiyuk's meeting with team brass, Shanahan said, he "could[n't] care less" about the outcome and that "your guess is as good as mine."

San Francisco 49ers wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk stands on the sideline before a game against the Los Angeles Rams in Inglewood, Calif., Sunday, Sept. 22, 2024. Getty Images

Then there is the matter of Aiyuk's legal trouble. There remains an outstanding warrant for his arrest in Santa Clara County, stemming from a video he posted to social media showing himself driving in excess of 100 mph down the road past Levi's Stadium.

Asked by The Post if Aiyuk had settled matters with police, Rosenhaus responded, "We're working on that."

Aiyuk also must come to an agreement with the 49ers on the $23 million bonus he received when he signed his extension in 2024. According to multiple reports Friday, San Francisco is demanding that he pay back $13 million of it before reinstating him.

The 49ers voided the remaining guaranteed money in Aiyuk's contract, citing his absence from the team facility while rehabbing the knee injury he suffered the last time he took the field for the 49ers.

Aiyuk tore his ACL and MCL seven weeks into his fifth NFL season. Since being drafted 25th overall out of Arizona State in 2020, Aiyuk had totaled 4,305 receiving yards and hauled in 25 touchdowns on 294 career receptions.

But he hasn't caught a pass — or stepped onto an NFL field — in more than two years.

San Francisco 49ers wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk (11) runs against the Los Angeles Rams in the first half of an NFL football game in Santa Clara, Calif., Oct. 18, 2020. AP

On Friday, he took the first step to getting back on the field.

It just isn't likely to happen in a 49ers uniform.

#2961. Kristaps Porziņģis says he will 'step away' from basketball in wake of lingering health issues

Kristaps Porziņģis is stepping away from the NBA while dealing with ongoing health issues, the veteran center announced Friday.

In a statement on social media, Porziņģis said he was not able to play at the level he hoped and has decided to focus on his health.

"This season I was really looking forward to running it with this group, but as I ramped up, my body didn't respond the way I anticipated," Porziņģis wrote. "I'm doing good, but I know what it takes, and I'm not where I want to be to perform at the level that I expect from myself. At this point, I've made the decision to prioritize my health and step away from the game I love."

Porzingis, who signed a two-year, $40 million contract with the Golden State Warriors this summer, said he hopes to work with the Warriors to "find a solution that makes sense" going forward. The center has been indefinitely away from the team this season while dealing with his health.

ESPN reported Wednesday the Warriors have not paid Porzingis any of his $20 million annual salary while he contemplated his NBA future. Porzingis has reportedly not contested the lack of payments.

Porzingis was acquired by the Warriors before the trade deadline last February and signed the extension to stay in Golden State in June. However, a personal "health issue" caused him to miss the start of training camp.

Neither Porzingis nor the Warriors has clarified what the center has been dealing with. But Porzingis has been open about his struggles with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS), a chronic condition that can affect the nervous system. Last fall, Porzingis revealed his POTS symptoms flared up during the 2025 NBA playoffs, when he was with the Boston Celtics, and severely limited his ability to play.

Porzingis said last month he was "getting better and better" after consulting with doctors, though he called the health issues "frustrating." It is unclear if Friday's announcement is a full retirement, or just a pause in his NBA career.

Nicknamed "The Unicorn" for his unique big-man skillset, the 31-year-old Latvian center steps away from basketball after an 11-year career spread across six teams. Porzingis is a 2024 NBA champion with the Celtics and earned an All-Star nod in 2018.

#2960. Warriors Get Concerning Injury News on 3 Key Players Amid Win Streak

SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA - OCTOBER 23: Draymond Green #23 of the Golden State Warriors stands on the court during their game against the Memphis Grizzlies at Chase Center on October 23, 2026 in San Francisco, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and/or using this photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Ezra Shaw/Getty Images) Getty

LOS ANGELES, CALIFORNIA - OCTOBER 21: Gui Santos #15 of the Golden State Warriors battles Sandro Mamukelashvili #54 of the Los Angeles Lakers for position during the second half of a game at Crypto.com Arena on October 21, 2026 in Los Angeles, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Sean M. Haffey/Getty Images) Getty

The Golden State Warriors have spent the past two weeks making their mounting injury problems look manageable. Their latest injury report suggests that could become considerably harder.

Draymond Green, Gary Payton II and Gui Santos are all questionable for Saturday's game against the Orlando Magic at Chase Center, according to the NBA's official injury report released Friday.

Green is dealing with right elbow inflammation and soreness, Payton has right calf tightness, and Santos continues to manage left knee tendinitis.

The uncertainty arrives as Golden State rides a six-game winning streak following a 0-3 start, with coach Steve Kerr potentially facing another round of rotation adjustments.

Getty Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors stands on the court during their game against the Memphis Grizzlies at Chase Center on October 23, 2026 in San Francisco, California.

Green's status is perhaps the most consequential development.

The four-time NBA champion has anchored Golden State's undersized frontcourt while serving as its defensive organizer and primary facilitator alongside Stephen Curry.

He delivered another example Thursday, recording 11 assists, five rebounds, a block and a steal in the Warriors' 120-100 victory over the Miami Heat.

Green scored only two points, but his impact extended well beyond his shooting numbers.

Kerr has repeatedly emphasized that the veteran's scoring is not a priority, particularly given his responsibilities defensively.

The Warriors have increasingly deployed Green at center, relying on his positioning and communication to compensate for their lack of traditional size.

His potential absence would leave Al Horford and Charles Bassey with greater responsibility inside.

Green has already missed two games this season, and his latest designation raises the possibility of a third.

GettyGui Santos of the Golden State Warriors battles Sandro Mamukelashvili of the Los Angeles Lakers for position during the second half of a game at Crypto.com Arena on October 21, 2026 in Los Angeles, California.

Golden State also faces uncertainty along the perimeter.

Payton's right calf soreness threatens to remove one of Kerr's most dependable point-of-attack defenders.

The veteran guard gives Golden State a disruptive presence against opposing ballhandlers, often creating transition opportunities through his defensive pressure.

Without him, De'Anthony Melton and Brandon Williams could receive additional minutes.

Santos, meanwhile, is attempting to return after missing the Warriors' previous two games.

The Brazilian forward participated in Friday's practice and was feeling better, according to Nick Friedell of The Athletic .

That represents progress after left knee tendinitis forced him to miss Golden State's victories over Phoenix and Miami.

Santos is averaging 7.2 points, four rebounds and 1.3 assists through six appearances.

Will Richard started Thursday in his absence and could remain in the lineup if Santos cannot play.

The three questionable designations add to an injury report that already includes several significant absences.

Jimmy Butler remains sidelined following right ACL surgery, while Moses Moody continues recovering from left patellar tendon surgery.

Kristaps Porzingis is also out after announcing Friday that he would step away from basketball to prioritize his health.

Despite those setbacks, Golden State has climbed to 6-3 behind Curry's scoring and a productive bench.

The Warriors' reserves outscored Miami's 53-27 on Thursday, helping extend a winning streak that has transformed their early-season outlook.

Orlando arrives with injury problems of its own.

Paolo Banchero, Jalen Suggs and Jonathan Isaac are among five Magic players already ruled out for Saturday.

The Warriors will have an opportunity to continue their surge against a depleted opponent.

Whether they can do so with Green, Payton and Santos remains uncertain.

Their final availability will help determine just how much depth Kerr must call upon Saturday night.

#2959. Warriors' Kristaps Porzingis issues update on his health, NBA future

Golden State Warriors center Kristaps Porziņģis, who has not been with the team as he addresses long-term health concerns, announced he will step away from basketball.

"This season I was really looking forward to running it with this group, but as I ramped up, my body didn't respond the way I anticipated," Porziņģis wrote Friday, Nov. 6 in a statement posted to social media.

"I'm doing good, but I know what it takes, and I'm not where I want to be to perform at the level that I expect from myself. At this point, I've made the decision to prioritize my health and step away from the game I love."

It's worth noting that Porziņģis didn't outright mention retirement in the statement, which could potentially leave a return to the sport down the road, should his health improve.

Porziņģis, 31, has been dealing with an undisclosed illness that has prevented him from joining the Warriors. He had missed all of training camp in late September and has been away from the team. Warriors coach Steve Kerr said last month that Porziņģis would be in the team facility for a check-in with Golden State's sports medicine and performance team.

Previously, stretching back to Porziņģis' final season with the Celtics in 2024-25, he had expressed frustrations over repeated absences that were caused from an undiagnosed condition that prevented him from having the energy and stamina to play.

Eventually, Porziņģis disclosed that he was dealing with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS) autoimmune symptoms, though he has not offered specific updates in recent months.

The Celtics traded Porziņģis to the Hawks in June 2025. Just eight months later, Atlanta then shipped Porziņģis in a separate deal to the Warriors, where he played only 15 games for the team.

ESPN reported Wednesday, Nov. 4 that Porziņģis was not being paid his $20 million salary for failing to render services to the team.

A one-time All-Star, Porziņģis is known for stretching the floor with his shooting range, while providing a rim-protecting presence on the other end. Across his 10-year career, Porziņģis has averaged 19.5 points, 7.6 rebounds and 1.9 assists per game in 533 appearances.

"In this situation, I want to show the utmost respect for the Golden State Warriors and work with them to find a solution that makes sense," Porziņģis continued in his statement. "I'm thankful to my family and close ones for always being there. Much appreciation to all of you for the support. Onwards and upwards."

#2957. Top House Oversight Democrat warns Trump family to 'lawyer up'

Rep. Robert Garcia, the California Democrat poised to lead the powerful House Oversight Committee, doubled down on his warning to President Donald Trump and his allies Friday, releasing a video promising sweeping investigations into the administration.

"Oversight Democrats are ready to take on the crooks, the con men and the Trump crime family. Lawyer up," the California Democrat wrote on X.

"The American people have spoken, and they are tired of the unprecedented level of corruption coming out of this White House and under President Trump," Garcia said in the video, which ends with the words "LAWYER UP" splashed across the screen.

The warning comes three days after Democrats' sweeping midterm victories handed them control of the House. When the new Congress convenes in January, Democrats will gain control of the committee's subpoena power, allowing them to compel testimony and obtain documents in investigations that could target Trump, his family and his allies.

Garcia said the committee would investigate what he described as potential corruption involving the Trump family's business dealings, cryptocurrency ventures and Pentagon contracts, as well as alleged abuses by Immigration and Customs Enforcement. He also vowed to scrutinize individuals he said were connected to convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

"Oversight Democrats are absolutely going to ensure that we investigate every single one of these instances," Garcia said. "Now let's get to work."

Garcia's approach may conflict with what some advisors are telling Democrats — to go after corporate interests rather than Trump and his family.

Garcia, currently the committee's top Democrat, formally launched his bid to become chairman Thursday. He is widely expected to secure the position.

The White House has dismissed Democrats' planned investigations as politically motivated, setting up a potentially contentious showdown between the Trump administration and the incoming Democratic congressional majority.

The White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

#2956. Cavs thump 76ers in James return, Celtics crush Hornets

The Cleveland Cavaliers spoiled LeBron James's latest homecoming on Friday, beating Philadelphia 119-95 to snap the 76ers' five-game NBA winning streak.

James, playing his first game in Cleveland since opting to head to Philadelphia as a free agent in June rather than returning for a third stint with his hometown Cavs, was greeted by warm cheers -- and scattered boos -- when he was introduced.

The four-time NBA champion, playing his 24th NBA season at the age of 41, was cheered again when he exited the game late in the fourth quarter.

In between it was a rough night for James and his Sixers teammates, who suffered just their second defeat of the young season.

The league's all-time leading scorer finished with eight points on three-of-12 shooting with seven rebounds, three assists, two steals and six turnovers.

Star center Joel Embiid missed all nine of his shots from the floor, making six free throws for six points.

Jaylen Brown, like James an off-season import, led the Sixers with 20 points, but the Cavaliers seized control early and repelled every Philadelphia surge to halt their two-game slide.

Donovan Mitchell scored 10 of his 23 points in the first quarter. Evan Mobley added 22 points, and Cleveland out-rebounded Philadelphia 56-42 despite the injury absence of center Jarrett Allen.

"I did not feel in a good rhythm offensively," James said. "We didn't look like much offensively. We had a couple of spurts. We were able to cut (the deficit) to three in the third quarter. But too many turnovers on my behalf."

Friday's games were part of NBA Cup group-stage action. The New York Knicks won the in-season tournament last year before going on to win their first NBA title since 1973.

Group-stage Cup games will be played throughout November, all counting in the regular-season standings. Quarter-finals will be played December 4-5 and semi-finals December 8-9 before the final on December 11 at Hinkle Fieldhouse in Indianapolis, Indiana.

The Boston Celtics drained a whopping 24 three-pointers on 44 attempts in a 119-80 blowout of the Hornets in Charlotte.

Jayson Tatum, back after sitting out Wednesday's win over the Bucks, drilled five three-pointers on the way to a game-high 34 points for Boston -- who led by as many as 46 points in the wire-to-wire win.

"I thought every shot he took was a great shot," Celtics coach Joe Mazzulla said of Tatum, who added eight rebounds and nine assists.

The Oklahoma City Thunder continued their dominance of the Grizzlies with a 128-97 win in Memphis.

Ajay Mitchell scored a career-high 35 points and two-time defending NBA Most Valuable Player Shai Gilgeous-Alexander added 28 for the Thunder, who notched a 13th straight regular-season win over the Grizzlies despite missing injured starters Jaylen Williams, Cason Wallace and Chet Holmgren.

The absences were irrelevant as the Thunder jumped to a 71-49 halftime lead.

Memphis dropped their third straight defeat after opening the season with six straight wins.

In Dallas, Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving came alive in the second half to help the Mavericks beat the Utah Jazz 112-100.

Dallas forward P.J. Washington scored 22 of his 26 points in a first half that ended tied 57-57.

Flagg, who scored four points before the break, finished with 21 and Irving, held to two points in the first half, finished with 14 as Dallas used a big third quarter to gain control and held on down the stretch.

#2955. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#2953. Inside the Rams’ secret shadow boxing league that is bringing the team together

Referees, ranked contenders, a leaderboard, and even a commissioner are overseeing the entire operation.

Welcome to the Los Angeles Rams shadow boxing league, where the competition is fierce, the trash talk is plentiful and the bragging rights might be worth more than the championship itself.

Oh, and at the center of it all is the reigning NFL Defensive Player of the Year, who also happens to be the undisputed champion of the league.

Instead of retreating into their phones between meetings, players are challenging teammates, laughing, talking trash and building relationships that extend beyond their position groups. Getty Images

What initially started as a harmless way to kill some time between meetings has blossomed into a full-fledged locker room phenomenon, bringing players together during a season filled with injuries, adversity and enormous expectations.

Rams linebacker Grant Stuard is the self-appointed commissioner, who has turned a childhood-style game into something resembling the UFC.

"We thought it'd be cool to get some organization going and get a league started," Stuard told the California Post in an exclusive interview Thursday.

Stuard and teammate Matthew Caldwell helped establish a running leaderboard that now features a top 10, position-group rankings and honorable mentions. Even injured players have found their way onto the board.

What initially started as a harmless way to kill some time between meetings has blossomed into a full-fledged locker room phenomenon. Getty Images

But at this point you're probably asking yourself the same question we did:

Despite its name, nobody is throwing any actual punches.

Two players stand face-to-face. One points up, down, left or right while the other simultaneously moves his head in a direction, hoping to avoid looking where his opponent points.

If the movements match, the attacker scores. Players alternate turns, attempting to anticipate each other's reactions and string together successful moves.

It's part reflexes, part mind games and entirely addictive. Throughout the season, easily over half the Rams locker room has participated. And because NFL players apparently can't compete at anything without turning it into its own organized sport, Stuard has established official rules.

Wide receiver Davante Adams, left, celebrates his touchdown with Puka Nacua. AP Photo/Scott Strazzante

Ranked matches are best-of-three and require a witness or referee. Matchups involving the top three competitors require two officials.

"If it's a top-three ranked match, you need two refs," Stuard explained. "You have to have some standards of what's acceptable play."

The rankings operate similar to the UFC's weight class rankings. An unranked player can challenge a ranked contender, and if the higher-ranked player accepts and loses, the leaderboard gets reshuffled.

Throughout the past several weeks rankings 2-10 have been erased more times than a toddler's Etch A Sketch, but one name has remained untouchable at the top of the leaderboard.

The best defensive player in the NFL today is also the reigning, defending, and undisputed king of the Rams shadow boxing league.

If and when the tournament takes place, Garrett's championship crown could finally be on the line. Abigail Dean /Los Angeles Rams

We've watched Garrett compete several times inside the locker room, and the results have been downright embarrassing for his challengers. Every matchup ends with Garrett victorious.

His reaction time is ridiculous, his anticipation uncanny and his ability to read opponents almost unfair.

"He has a great combination of reactive defense, but also very accurate offense," Stuard said. "He picks up on mannerisms and patterns."

Rams linebacker Shaun Dolac, who recently challenged Garrett himself and lost, agreed.

The Los Angeles Rams defense lines up pre-snap during an NFL football game against the Los Angeles Chargers. Icon Sportswire via Getty Images

"He's just quick," Dolac told the California Post. "He's got the reaction time. He's got everything. He's got the moves."

But the real victory isn't your name on the leaderboard, it's the unattended byproduct that comes from the competitions and excitement around the league itself.

Instead of retreating into their phones between meetings, players are challenging teammates, laughing, talking trash and building relationships that extend beyond their position groups.

"It's another way of competing other than on the field," Dolac said. "It's a way to kind of decompress as well."

"You're not just sitting around on your phone. You're still interacting with each other."

And for a Rams team that stumbled out to a 2-4 start before battling back to 4-4, those connections matter. Getty Images

The shadow boxing league joins playing cards, grounding walks, and date nights as ways to develop off the field chemistry and team bonding. There's also a precedent for this translating into something meaningful.

Last season, the Seattle Seahawks embraced their own shadow boxing craze, with players crediting the game for helping build relationships across position groups. That connectedness became part of the culture surrounding Seattle's eventual Super Bowl championship.

Throughout the season, easily over half the Rams locker room has participated. Getty Images

Nobody should confuse shadow boxing with blocking schemes or defensive adjustments. The Rams won't win a championship because Garrett can outmaneuver a teammate in a pointing contest.

But championships aren't built exclusively on Sundays, either. They're built through trust, relationships, and the willingness to fight and play for the guy standing next to you.

And for a Rams team that stumbled out to a 2-4 start before battling back to 4-4, those connections matter.

Stuard also revealed that the Rams shadow boxing league is about to get even more serious. The commissioner is planning a tournament next week, potentially featuring seeded matchups, wild cards, and first-round byes based on the rankings.What is this, the College Football Playoff?

If and when the tournament takes place, Garrett's championship crown could finally be on the line.

Good luck to whomever draws that matchup.

#2952. The latest injury update on Iowa basketball's Drew McKeever

IOWA CITY — Saint Mary's transfer Drew McKeever is still yet to make his Iowa basketball debut.

He was unavailable for Iowa basketball's matchup with Eastern Illinois on Nov. 6, meaning that he missed the first two games of the 2026-27 season due to a back injury.

McKeever took part in pregame warmups against Lipscomb before ultimately being ruled out. But McKeever wasn't in uniform and did not participate in pregame warmups prior to the contest against Eastern Illinois.

"He's progressing," McCollum said of McKeever after Iowa's 91-47 win over Eastern Illinois. "We did think he was going to play (against Eastern Illinois). He just kind of had some more spasms in his back a little bit yesterday. So, we're hopeful that it turns, but I think backs are tricky. One day he can feel good, and the next day, it's awful. So we're hopeful, because he's really good. In practice, he was as dominant as we've had. So, we'll see if we can get him back."

Even without McKeever, Iowa been getting frontcourt production. Trevin Jirak has been very good so far this season and flirted with a triple-double against Eastern Illinois, posting eight points, eight assists and seven rebounds.

But the 7-foot-3 McKeever would give Iowa another dimension. McKeever was a big-time addition from the transfer portal this offseason. He averaged 8.2 points, 9.2 rebounds and 1.8 assists at Saint Mary's last season.

Iowa's schedule is about to heat up. The Hawkeyes are now set to face three power-conference opponents in a row: Virginia Tech, Creighton and Xavier. That stretch begins with Virginia Tech on Nov. 10 in Sioux City.

#2951. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2950. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2949. Zach LaVine is the latest opponent to heap praise on Luka Dončić

As Luka Dončić sets the league aflame with an unparalleled scoring run to open the season, he's leaving a trail of awestruck opponents in his wake.

When he dropped 42 points on the Heat at the start of the week, Giannis Antetokounmpo was certain to make sure his standing in the league all-time, not just current, was known.

"When you have a guy like Luka, obviously he's one of the best scorers to ever play this game, if not the best," Giannis said before a reporter added a "right now" at the end of his statement. "Not right now, not right now. Of all time. I feel like some people are scared to say that but it's gotta be up there."

A few days later, Luka scored 42 against the Kings, prompting a similar compliment from Zach LaVine.

As a brief aside, LaVine had also had a funny interaction with Austin Reaves during his postgame presser after Reaves accidentally walked into the wrong room.

As for Luka, his performances are hard to properly describe at this point. He's putting up numbers that we haven't seen in a long time. The only way to properly qualify what he's doing is to put it in an all-time context, because no one in the league has done this before.

And that's how both Giannis and LaVine framed it. Granted, NBA players often aren't the best at putting things in proper context — look no further than their All-Star voting — but they're also the only ones with firsthand experience.

If Luka is leaving them repeating a similar sort of sentiment, then it's hard not to agree.
