#2948. Las Vegas Raiders-San Francisco 49ers FEED Topic: Who gets more touches between Mike Washington Jr., Ashton Jeanty?

Las Vegas Raiders head coach Klint Kubiak made headlines during last week's game against the New York Jets. Not for himself, but a decision he made with Las Vegas' backfield.

Kubiak didn't just switch the Raiders' lead running back between rookie Mike Washington Jr. and second-year pro Ashton Jeanty; he turned Washington into the feature back with 25 carries and made Jeanty the third-down option with just two carries and three catches.

Now, heading into the Week 9 matchup against the San Francisco 49ers, the question arises of whether the workload split between Washington and Jeanty will continue.

Some have speculated that Kubiak's decision was punitive-based. Ahead of last week's game, Jeanty gave the Jets 'bulletin board material' by saying that a few players on New York's defense "don't really want to tackle". While the first-time head coach is still establishing his identity, he's made it clear that he takes a 'no-nonsense' type of approach and doesn't divulge much to the media to avoid situations like this. So, there's reason to believe that the running back's comments irked Kubiak and played a role in the former's workload last Sunday.

However, this Sunday will give us a better answer to see if the 25-to-2 (or 12.5-to-1) ratio of carries, or a similar type of split, continues. On Monday, the head coach indicated that the workload between the two backs could vary game to game.

"I think it's always a moving target," he replied when asked if the Raiders will have a shared backfield moving forward. "See where your team's at, and who's playing the best ball at the time. And it's a 17-game regular season where things change every week. And they've been changing at guard…That's the NFL.

"We have a young roster, and we're going to give guys opportunities and see what fits us best. But your 11, whether you're playing really well or not playing well at all, it's always going to change a little bit."

Despite those comments, it's hard to deny that the Raiders' rushing attack did a better job of staying on schedule last week.

Washington had 83 yards on his 25 carries, which comes out to 3.3 ypc. That's pretty comparable to Jeanty's 3.1 ypc for the season. But the rookie's average was brought down by a handful of stuffed runs near the goal line. Also, Vegas' EPA per rush was 0.11 points better last week than the team's season average, and its success rate on the ground was 7.2% higher, per Sumer Sports.

That being said, Washington's lack of success in the red zone can't be ignored. He's averaging 1.2 ypc (17 attempts for 21 yards) inside the 20-yard line, per Sumer, and is viewed as more of a home-run hitter out of the backfield. Granted, Jeanty isn't much better in the scoring area — 1.6 ypc (22 attempts for 36 yards) — but it is an improvement on a slightly bigger sample size. Also, he's arguably the better option at getting the tough yards of the two backs.

So, maybe Klint Kubiak's formula moving forward is to have the rookie help get the offense into the red zone and then have the second-year pro take over with fresh legs. Of course, we won't know the answer until Sunday's game, which is why the topic is this week's game day FEED discussion.

Meanwhile, the 49ers have a very good defense that is great at stopping the run. San Francisco ranks fifth in the NFL with 89.0 rushing yards allowed per game and is second in EPA per rush allowed at negative 0.17. Especially since the Jets were missing their best run defender last week, defensive tackle T'Vondre Sweat, this weekend will be a true test to see if the Raiders' rushing attack improves with Washington as the lead back.

So, for this week's game day discussion on The FEED, we'll be keeping tabs on how the carries are divided up in Las Vegas' backfield. The game kicks off at 1:25 p.m. PST and will air on FOX (check your local listings). I look forward to talkin' some ball with y'all!

#2947. OpenAI, Arm and Qualcomm executives react to post-midterm AI sell-off

As a post-midterm rally Wednesday turned into a steep sell-off Thursday, tech executives sought to look through the notion that potential data center constraints could be catastrophic for their businesses.

In response to a Democratic sweep of Congress as well as a sizeable number of state governorships and legislatures in the midterm election Tuesday night, stocks initially rallied Wednesday, posting their best day in more than 18 months as President Trump accepted the blowout results with little fanfare.

The tide quickly turned Thursday, however, with stocks selling off sharply as investors feared a potential slowdown in the data center build-out after wins by candidates in favor of data center regulation in states like Texas and the possibility that the artificial intelligence trade may be past its peak. Tech led the sell-off, with the "Magnificent Seven" losing over $1 trillion in aggregate market cap.

Stocks recovered some of their losses Friday.

Here's how tech executives reacted to the midterms and sell-off threat:

Haas characterized the postelection fear as "very, very short term thinking" and not likely to be a major threat to Arm's business in the long term, but said the U.S. must keep investing in the data center build-out before the rest of the world catches up.

"If the U.S. hesitates in any way, shape, or form in terms of data center build-out, the rest of the world is ready and willing. There is just huge demand for this capacity," Haas told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Thursday.

Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen Friday that OpenAI will continue investing in data center infrastructure after voters resoundly rejected data centers in their backyards during the midterm election. The company will also focus on community concerns, including electricity. "We will never raise your electricity bill," Friar said."When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand. It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital," Friar said.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them."

Amon said the anxiety about the future of the data center build-out doesn't pose a significant threat to the chipmaker's business, as data center infrastructure is just one segment. Qualcomm slid 9.49% in Thursday's sell-off before recovering some losses Friday.

"The whole market is down, but I think the Qualcomm story is a little bit different," Amon told CNBC's Jon Fortt Thursday. Unlike traditional PC and data center chipmakers, Qualcomm has historically focused on low-power consumption computing. "The story of Qualcomm is what we have done outside that on the data center specific question."

Levie told "Squawk on the Street" Thursday that productivity gains meant AI "is clearly going in one direction," but said a tendency for industry messaging to coalesce around "doomerism" can exacerbate the public perception problems AI companies face.

"I think it's a little bit easier to rotate it toward maybe the more risk-oriented messages, and that's something that our industry needs to work on clearly. But ultimately, obviously, the consumer needs to see the direct benefit, and that's something that will just take time," said Box's Levie.

Risher said the dour market sentiment wasn't concerning for Lyft's business, as consumer sentiment and bookings remained strong. "I wouldn't get too distracted about the short term stuff of what's going on in the market. If I look at the consumer, for example, we just had the biggest Halloween we've ever had," Risher told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Friday.

#2945. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2942. OpenAI will keep investing in AI data centers despite election results, CFO tells CNBC

OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar on Friday said the company will continue to invest in artificial intelligence infrastructure, even after voters strongly opposed data centers during Tuesday's midterm elections.

"When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand," Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen in an interview at the Citi TMT Leadership Summit in Pebble Beach, California. "It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital."

Out of 41 local ballot measures across 10 states, voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures in all but one, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday. Opposition to the facilities mounted swiftly over the summer as Americans grew worried about their potential to harm the environment, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss.

Investors have turned skeptical on the AI trade of late due to a combination of higher borrowing costs, concerns about soaring debt and public backlash toward the industry. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, led by a plunge in shares of the chipmakers and neocloud companies most closely tied to the AI buildout. The stocks rebounded some on Friday.

OpenAI depends on data centers to train its AI models and serve customers, and Friar said she doesn't think the election results dramatically changed anything for the company. On Thursday, OpenAI published a policy blueprint and a series of commitments that it pledged to apply to company-owned and -operated data centers in the future.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them," Friar said.

OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, and Friar said Friday that the company would continue to look into a 2027 debut. Anthropic, OpenAI's chief rival, is trying to go public first, with an aim to hit the Nasdaq in mid-November.

Anthropic's revenue in the first nine months of the year jumped roughly 1,200% to $33.2 billion, according to its prospectus. The company said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

OpenAI is in talks to raise funding at a $1.25 billion pre-money valuation, Friar said Friday. She said "a good CFO always raises money when she can" and that the round is ongoing.

The company has closed an initial $11 billion portion of that funding round, CNBC confirmed on Friday, and it could raise up to an additional $9 billion in the coming weeks. Bloomberg was first to report the $11 billion portion.

"The demand for AI is real," Friar said. "I don't think anyone's arguing with that, or if they are, I think it's a losing argument."

#2941. MLB announces Pete Crow-Armstrong, Cody Bellinger as Platinum Glove winners in Friday night news dump

Pete Crow-Armstrong and Cody Bellinger have won MLB's Platinum Glove awards, meant to honor the top overall defender in each league. It's unclear if the league actually wants people to know that.

For reasons that remain clear only to MLB, the 2026 Platinum Gloves were announced at 11 p.m. ET on a Friday night, with the Chicago Cubs and New York Yankees taking home an award that is decided by an easily swayed fan vote. It is the first time either player has won the award.

The pair seems happy with their new hardware.

The Platinum Gloves are selected from among the 10 Gold Glovers in each league. This year, those were:

Pitcher: Tanner Bibee, Cleveland Guardians

First base: Christian Walker, Houston Astros

Second base: Cole Young, Seattle Mariners

Left field: Cody Bellinger, New York Yankees

Center field: Ceddane Rafaela, Boston Red Sox

Right field: Wilyer Abreu, Boston Red Sox

Catcher: Gabriel Moreno, Arizona Diamondbacks

Second base: Brice Turang, Milwaukee Brewers

Shortstop: Geraldo Perdomo, Arizona Diamondbacks

Left field: Jackson Chourio, Milwaukee Brewers

Center field: Pete Crow-Armstrong, Chicago Cubs

Right field: Corbin Carroll, Arizona Diamondbacks

The timing of the announcement was nothing new, nor was the methodology. The winner is decided by combining a fan vote held by Rawlings with "votes" from a stat called the SABR Defensive Index (SDI).

Bellinger led the AL in SDI, while Crow-Armstrong ranked second behind only Milwaukee Brewers second baseman Brice Turang, another Gold Glover. As for other advanced defensivee stats, Crow-Armstrong ranked first in his league in Statcast's Fielding Run Value and third in Defensive Runs Saved. Bellinger was 21st and second in those stats, respectively.

It is, of course, extremely difficult to compare defenders across different positions, as these stats are trying to figure out how, say, an outfielder with a penchant for difficult catches compares to a catcher who can throw out runners and frame pitches. It's an inherent flaw of the Platinum Gloves, which would be more of a problem if anyone took them seriously.

At the very least, the Platinum Glove is another feather in the cap this season for Crow-Armstrong, who is widely expected to win NL MVP next week. The Cubs star posted MLB's seventh 40-40 season this year, making him an elite player on both offense and defense this season.

Bellinger, meanwhile, received his first All-Star nod since winning NL MVP in 2019 and was among the Yankees' top players this season after returning to the team last winter on a five-year, $162.5 million contract.

Rawling also announced the Toronto Blue Jays and Chicago Cubs as the winners of the Team Gold Gloves. The Cubs ranked third in MLB this season in defensive efficiency (i.e. the rate at which they turned balls in play into outs), while the Blue Jays were 13th. Toronto, which had no Gold Glovers, also led the AL in errors.

#2940. Sen. Chris Murphy released from hospital after stroke, will miss votes for 'several weeks' as he rehabs

Connecticut Democratic Sen. Chris Murphy said Friday he had been released from a hospital after suffering a stroke last weekend.

Murphy, 53, said he will be in a rehabilitation center for the next "several weeks," meaning he will miss votes in the Senate when Congress returns on Monday.

"The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I've made so far," he said in a statement.

"In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today," said Murphy, who has been discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028.

Dr. Jose Suarez, chief of Neurosciences Critical Care at The Johns Hopkins Hospital, in a statement released by Murphy's office, said, "Senator Murphy experienced a small hemorrhagic stroke."

"He has shown tremendous progress, and at the current rate of improvement, we expect that he will make a full recovery following rehabilitation," Suarez said.

Murphy had been in the hospital since having a stroke at his son's hockey game.

"Last Saturday afternoon, I felt something was not right with my body and called 9-1-1 out of an abundance of caution, and I'm glad I did," Murphy said in his statement. "I was diagnosed with a small bleed in the thalamus region of my brain."

Murphy's stroke came three days before Democratic candidates, in a stunning election result, won more than enough races to flip the Senate and House of Representatives from Republican majority control in January.

Murphy, who was not up for reelection this year, before the election had campaigned for Democratic candidates around the country, most recently in Michigan, Minnesota and Florida. He had been scheduled to campaign in New Hampshire on Sunday with Rep. Chris Pappas, who on Tuesday won election to the Senate.

#2937. 'I'd be stunned' if Philip Rivers isn't NC State's next coach, Alabama sports reporter tells ABC11

A longtime, award-winning Alabama sports reporter who has closely covered Philip Rivers' high school coaching career says he would be "stunned" if the former NC State quarterback does not become the Wolfpack's next head coach, with timing appearing to be the biggest remaining question.

Ben Thomas of AL.com has covered Rivers since his arrival at St. Michael Catholic in 2021 as head football coach. He discussed Rivers' future during an interview with ABC11's Travon Miles as speculation continues to grow around the former NFL quarterback potentially returning to Raleigh.

"I think for the last couple of weeks, I've thought Philip Rivers is going to be the coach at North Carolina State at some point," Thomas said. "I mean, I think the question right now is when does that happen? When is our announcement made?"

WATCH: Philip Rivers speaks after high school team's season ends

Speculation about an announcement from NC State on its next football coach will ramp up after Philip Rivers' team was ousted Friday night in the state playoffs.

Thomas said Rivers met with NC State officials earlier in the week in Fairhope, where Rivers coaches the Cardinal.

"I'd be surprised if he's not the coach at NC State; I'm going to be stunned," Thomas said. "I think the timing, the question now is what's the timing."

Thomas said he first began to believe Rivers could be a serious candidate as soon as NC State moved on from former coach Dave Doeren.

Thomas said he had heard Rivers had withdrawn from serving as offensive coordinator for an Alabama all-star game scheduled for December. Shortly afterward, the NC State vacancy emerged.

"When I heard that Philip pulled out of that game and then a couple of days later, there was an opening at North Carolina State, that seemed that the pieces kind of seemed to add up," Thomas said.

While Rivers has publicly confirmed speaking with NC State, Thomas said the former quarterback has remained largely consistent in his approach.

"He did say a lot of nice things about how that place is different," Thomas said. "Obviously, love that place, you know, nothing's imminent, and we'll see where we go from here."

Rivers has said previously that he intended to coach his high school team through the postseason. That ended for St. Michael on Friday night when the Cardinal lost 42-35 in the playoffs to Glenwood School.

With his team's ouster, speculation is certain to ramp up on when or if an announcement could come from NC State.

"We'll see what the future holds," Rivers said after Friday night's game. He again confirmed that he met with NC State officials on Wednesday, but said it was just a step in the process.

Thomas said Rivers originally viewed high school coaching as his long-term goal after retiring from professional football. However, he said he believes Rivers' brief NFL return last year and subsequent head coaching interview with the Buffalo Bills helped broaden his coaching ambitions.

"Being a high school coach was not a publicity stunt for him," Thomas said. "That was his dream."

According to Thomas, Rivers later realized, "Something I know now is I can impact people, football players at any level, not just at the high school level."

Thomas also pointed to the role of Rivers' son, Gunner, who is committed to NC State.

"I think that's a huge part of it," Thomas said.

Asked whether the chance to coach his son in college was a factor in his decision, Rivers responded, "Oh, sure."

Rivers said he wouldn't call that "the factor, the deciding factor, but certainly, it would be special."

Thomas suggested the situation could be different if Gunner were committed elsewhere.

"If he was committed to Auburn or Alabama or somewhere, we'd be talking about a lot different situation," he said.

Having watched Rivers build St. Michael's football program, Thomas said the former NFL star possesses the energy, leadership style and competitiveness that have made him successful as a coach.

"He's so energetic," Thomas said, recalling Rivers' interactions with players. "He reminds the guys all the time that, hey, enjoy this."

Thomas also highlighted Rivers' ability to attract players and supporters.

"What the high school coaches have told me, and people know him say, is he'll be able to galvanize that fan base," Thomas said. "People want to contribute to that program."

Thomas added, "People want to come play football for Philip Rivers."

As for concerns about Rivers making the jump directly from high school football to a major college program, Thomas said he believes Rivers' leadership qualities and football background would translate.

"I think people want to play for him, and I think he's got the football knowledge and the competitive fire to be successful wherever he is," Thomas said.

Although Thomas acknowledged family considerations remain significant for Rivers, who has a large family and established roots in Alabama, he repeatedly returned to the same conclusion.

"I would be shocked if it didn't go that way at this point," Thomas said.

#2936. OpenAI bugging suspect Alfozan pleads not guilty

The Saudi national charged with planting a listening device in the headquarters of OpenAI while he worked there pleaded not guilty on Friday during an appearance in San Francisco federal court.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, was sent back to jail after the brief hearing. Alfozan has been held without bail since his arrest on a criminal complaint in early October.

He faces a maximum possible sentence of five years in prison if convicted in the case.

A grand jury indicted Alfozan on Oct. 27 on a single count of interception of oral communications, court records show.

Prosecutors say that he put a recording device in a television in an executive conference room at OpenAI's offices in San Francisco on Sept. 28.

Alfozan's lawyer declined to comment after the hearing before Magistrate Judge Peter Kang.

The judge ordered Alfozan to next appear in court on Nov. 17.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm, Raed Ventures.

"He currently serves as Engineering Lead at OpenAI, with a focus on AI Agents and the data platform," Alfozan's bio page on Raed's site says.

Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of Saudi Arabia's royal family, is a venture partner in the same firm.

Alfozan's case comes as U.S. companies such as OpenAI are making rapid advancements in developing artificial intelligence agents, and as concerns grow about the potential theft of AI tech from those companies.In June, President Donald Trump, in an executive order on AI innovation, said it was U.S. policy "to protect American ingenuity and intellectual property from exploitation and theft by adversaries; and to cultivate America's advanced AI-enabled capabilities."Trump has had a close relationship with Saudi Arabia. But he has also taken steps during his second term in the White House to limit the number of visas for highly skilled foreign workers.OpenAI earlier this year held discussions with Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the country's sovereign wealth fund, about investing in the company.

— Jordan Novet reported from San Francisco

#2935. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2934. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#2933. The Orlando Blueprint: What the Suns take into Houston and San Antonio

The Suns finally exhaled. One win, and a lot left to prove.

Thursday's 117-104 win over Orlando snapped a four-game losing streak, and after weeks of struggle, this fan base needed it just as much as the team did.

Now the hard part. Phoenix is 2-5, and the win came against a Magic team missing Paolo Banchero and Jalen Suggs and coming off two straight games under 100 points. Credit Phoenix for handling it on the second night of a back-to-back, but nobody is booking a playoff run off that.

This is the middle of a four-games-in-six-nights homestand that could make or break the season before it started. Portland, Houston, and San Antonio are three of the top six teams in the West. Orlando was supposed to be the "easy" one. Phoenix is 1-1 through the stretch, and the bar was 2-2. That means stealing one from the Rockets on Saturday or the Spurs right after. Easier said than done.

So forget the box score for a minute. The real value of the Orlando game is that Phoenix showed, for 48 minutes, what a winning version of this roster looks like. The question for this week is how much of it carries over.

Defense first, and it showed up in the turnover column

Orlando gave it away 25 times. Phoenix gave it away 14. That 11-turnover swing was the single biggest reason the Suns won. They pressured the ball, jumped passing lanes and turned stops into transition looks. Booker had 3 steals and Dillon Brooks had 2. When the defense gets set and forces mistakes, the offense gets easy shots and the whole night snowballs in the right direction.

Phoenix went 17-of-34 from deep. Orlando went 13-of-38. That is 12 extra points from distance on four fewer attempts. Those made up a 15-rebound deficit and 11 fewer free throws.

Devin Booker set the tone with 12 in the first quarter and 23 by halftime, finishing with 30 on 11-of-22 shooting, 8 assists, and a +16. Luke Kennard rescued the bench in the second quarter with 12 points on 3 threes. When Phoenix opened the third with sloppy turnovers, Brooks took the baton and finished with 24 points, 4 threes and a +12. Miles Bridges did the dirty work with 18 points, 13 rebounds and 5 assists.

What travels: the effort and the defensive intent. Ball pressure works against anybody. The Suns looked more engaged on that end than at any point during the losing streak, and that does not depend on who is across from them.

What probably doesn't: 50% from three. Phoenix is not shooting that every night, and a healthy, disciplined opponent will not hand over 25 turnovers. Strip those two numbers back toward normal and the Orlando game gets a lot closer.

What got exposed: the Suns were outrebounded by 15 and took 11 fewer free throws. Orlando went just 13-of-24 at the line and wasted the gap. Better teams will cash it in from the charity stripe.

It will take a complete, 48-minute effort to win these games. They had positive flashes that lasted 12-15 minutes in the previous three games leading up to the Orlando game, but were unable to put it all together and pull out wins.

The recipe is simple. Being active defensively, communicating relentlessly, and staying connected are the keys to winning games you aren't supposed to.

They won't beat Houston or San Antonio if they get killed on the glass or in the interior again. You get that leeway against Orlando. But the concept of winning a complete game like Orlando is a building block they can take into these next two games.

They may have to do it in a different way, but staying locked in for 48 minutes is the only way they stand a chance.

Keagan Dixon led the offense with 68 rushing yards on 8 carries.

GIBRALTAR – Two interceptions and a fumble were costly for Gibraltar Carlson in a close game.

Carlson's only points came on an 8-yard pass from Austyn Lawrence to Adrian Blackmon in the fourth quarter.

"I'm proud of how our players and coaches fought," Gendron said. "It was another good season for the Marauders. A special thank you goes out to our seniors for everything they contributed to the program over their four years."

#2931. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

#2928. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2925. A disturbing employment trend is hiding beneath the latest strong job numbers

Americans who lose their jobs these days are facing a harsh new problem: It's getting harder to find another one quickly.

The average jobless worker's bout of unemployment surpassed 27 weeks in October on a seasonally adjusted basis, according to Friday's jobs report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That amounts to a job search of nearly seven months, the longest outside of the pandemic period since 2016, when the U.S. labor market was finally getting back on track after the 2008 financial crisis.

About 2 million Americans have been without work for at least 27 weeks, the BLS reported. People in this category are referred to as the "long-term unemployed" and accounted for 27.6% of all jobless workers in October, the highest share since late 2021.

"This is a series that can be noisy from month to month," said Cory Stahle, senior economist at Indeed, a hiring platform. "But if you look over a longer time horizon, there is no mistake in the trend."

It's a troubling sign in an otherwise steady labor market. Workers stuck on the sidelines are a casualty of what's become a "low-hire, low-fire" job market, in which companies are hesitant to lay workers off, but aren't adding them either.

Research shows that Americans who experience long-term unemployment face depleted savings, lower earnings potential over time and declining mental health.

What's more, workers who are unemployed for extended periods risk falling into a self-perpetuating cycle, researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond wrote earlier this year.

"Time spent unemployed may itself reduce the probability of finding a job, perhaps because skills depreciate, networks thin or employers interpret long unemployment durations as a negative signal," the researchers warned.

Workers stop being able to claim most, if not all, government unemployment benefits after 26 weeks in a year. Given that, a rising share of long-term unemployed people can explain in part why initial and continuing jobless claims have remained low.

"Now, the average unemployed person takes longer to find a job than their benefits would last," Stahle said. "That is a really key point of this."

While jobless workers appear to be having a tough time, this week's employment data shows strength in other areas of the labor market.

Nonfarm payrolls blew past economists' forecasts for October and added 165,000 jobs, according to Friday's release. The unemployment rate ticked down to 4.1%, slightly lower than economists predicted.

Job openings came in much higher than economists expected in September, according to a separate report from the BLS released on Tuesday. The number of workers quitting jobs — a barometer of their confidence to obtain new employment — spiked to levels not seen since March 2025.

The BLS also said layoffs and discharges dropped to their lowest volume in more than a year. However, a recent string of high-profile workforce cuts could throw cold water on views among workers that the market is heating up.

CNBC reported this week that Disney is planning to axe thousands of jobs, the latest of several waves of layoffs at the media giant this year. American Express said last month that it was slashing head count by 5% as it reorients around artificial intelligence.

"It's difficult to separate what's happening to you or the people around you from the big picture," said Elizabeth Renter, lead economist at Life360. "When your LinkedIn is filled with layoffs and your social circle features a few folks who have been job seeking for several months, positive narratives about the labor market can seem jarring."

As if the Jets didn't face a big enough challenge in facing the 5-2 Chiefs on Sunday in Kansas City, they'll also be without Garrett Wilson.

Garrett Wilson of the New York Jets looks on during a game against the Miami Dolphins in East Rutherford, N.J., Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. Getty Images

That includes Adonai Mitchell and Omar Cooper Jr.

Mitchell is coming off a career-best game against Las Vegas, as he finished the loss with seven catches, 142 yards and a touchdown.

Cooper has impressed as a rookie since coming back from an ankle injury, but he didn't catch any passes last week after topping the 100-yard mark in each of the team's previous two games against New England and Miami.

"It's a great opportunity, but you always want a guy like that out there,'' Cooper said of missing Wilson. "It doesn't change much for my preparation and my game plan. I'm gonna do the same things as before and make sure I can attack whatever I can to help the team."

Certainly, though, it will likely increase the pressure on the receiving corps, as well as the team itself.

"That's possible," Cooper said. "But it's what you consider to be pressure."

New York Jets wide receiver Adonai Mitchell (15) stretches during practice in Florham Park, N.J., Friday, Oct. 23, 2026. Bill Kostroun for NY Post

Pressure could be trying to make up for missing a pass catcher who finished with over 1,000 yards receiving in his three healthy seasons and could be on his way to another this year after being limited to seven games in 2025.

Among the reasons Wilson has seen his targets decrease is the attention that's paid to him by opposing defenses.

New York Jets wide receiver Omar Cooper Jr. (11) runs with the ball against the Tennessee Titans during the first quarter at Nissan Stadium in Nashville, Tennessee, Sunday, Sept. 13, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

That luxury is gone for the Jets this week, at least, which will make it more difficult for Mitchell to duplicate the success he had against the Raiders.

Even Mitchell can't guarantee a repeat of last week.

"We'll see," Mitchell said of how he puts up those kinds of numbers consistently. "It was my first one. It's just not changing my preparation. Ultimately, the preparation is what got me the opportunity on Sunday. So keep that consistent and always keep the main thing the main thing."

Certainly, getting more accustomed to playing with Geno Smith helps.

"In my eyes, that's when preparation meets opportunity," Mitchell said of them working well together. "The preparation was put in during OTAs, during summer break and during fall camp. So it's just a product of preparation."

Offensive coordinator Frank Reich said Thursday, "In one respect, you can't replace Garrett Wilson. He's a unique player. But on the other hand, we're not afraid. We saw the game [Mitchell] had. We saw the game [Cooper] had. We have a lot of confidence in our guys. They're different players, so maybe you do slightly different things. … You have to play to your players' strengths."

Even if it's not always the players you rely on the most.

#2921. Fossil Ridge football season ends with playoff road loss

The Fossil Ridge High School football season ended in the Colorado Class 5A playoffs for a fifth time in six seasons.

No. 12 Regis Jesuit routed No. 21 Fossil 49-21 in the 5A first round Nov. 6 at Kellogg Stadium in Aurora.

The Raiders scored on their first five possessions and led 35-0 before Fossil got on the board late in the first half.

Heralded junior quarterback Luke Rubley threw five touchdowns, two to Colt Jones. That sparked a Regis offense which had faced tough sledding against top teams like Grandview and Valor Christians to close the regular season.

Regis moves on for a rematch against No. 5 Grandview in the 5A second round Nov. 13. The Raiders are now 4-0 all-time against the SaberCats, with all four wins coming since 2022.

Fossil Ridge couldn't get anything going until it was too late against a Denver-area power.

Fossil quarterback Colton Jantz capped off another strong campaign, throwing TD passes to Caleb Camping and fellow junior Gavin Zanotelli . Zanotelli caught another touchdown from running back Will Hough off a trick play to open the second half.

Even after Fossil forced an ensuing fumble, the momentum was short-lived. Regis Jesuit's Andrew Mihm intercepted Jantz and returned it for a pick-six score.

It was a disappointing exit for Fossil after showcasing major improvement in October.

Fossil Ridge won four of its last five regular season games following a 1-4 start, beating rival Rocky Mountain, upsetting Legacy and pushing unbeaten Fort Collins to the wire before defeating Erie and Rock Canyon to return to .500.

This was still a bounce back season for the SaberCats, who went 2-8 last year while missing the 5A playoffs for the first time since 2020.

With Jantz and top target Zanotelli headlining the likely returners next season, Fossil could make some more noise in 2027.

The reigning 3A runner-up is out of the playoffs after No. 15 Windsor couldn't hold a fourth-quarter in the 3A football first round Nov. 7.

No. 18 Silver Creek scored two touchdowns in the final six minutes, including the go-ahead touchdown pass with nine seconds left to leave with a 17-13 win.

The Wizards held a 13-3 lead in a defensive battle heading to the fourth quarter behind Robert Trotter Jr. TD tosses to Bekum Helm and Devonte Fontana.

But the Raptors clawed back, first with a rushing score to trim the lead.

After a stop, they drove down the field (with a fourth-down conversion) and sat inside the Windsor 10-yard line in the final moments. Braeden Macchia rolled right and found a wide-open Wyatt Mills for the winner.

Silver Creek (5-6 overall) will face No. 2 Holy Family in the 3A second round next week.

Windsor (5-6) had surged down the stretch, winning four of the last five regular season games after a 1-4 start and finishing 5-1 in league play.

But the Wizards couldn't keep it rolling, failing to win a playoff game in a season for the first time since 2021.

Chris Abshire covers high school and community sports for the Coloradoan.

#2919. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2917. Steelers’ Christian Kuntz named NFLPA community MVP

This article originally appeared on SteelersNOW.com.

Pittsburgh Steelers long snapper Christian Kuntz has won the NFLPA Community MVP Award for his community work in his hometown of Pittsburgh.

Kuntz led two community events in one week. The Steelers' long snapper donated $10,000 to Pittsburgh Morrow school to help open a new resource room, which will provide free shoes, uniforms, winter jackets, hygiene products, and food for more than 200 students and 70 economically disadvantaged families each month.

That same week, his Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation hosted its first Halloween Bash, welcoming 80 local kids for trick-or-treating, pumpkin decorating, food, and a chance to pick out a free costume.

With Kuntz being named Community MVP, the NFLPA is donating $10,000 to the Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation.

The Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation was started in 2023 by Kuntz. His foundation is dedicated to promoting youth leadership and engagement, fostering mentoring relationships, and supporting underserved communities.

#2916. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2915. Lakers’ new big man accepts JJ Redick’s challenge, takes team to new heights

Luka Dončić is dominating headlines with arguably the greatest start in NBA history. But it's no secret that Walker Kessler's blue-collar work in the paint is just as essential to the Lakers' success this season.

Before the Lakers played the Clippers on Oct. 28, coach JJ Redick issued a challenge to Kessler after he fell short of grabbing double-digit rebounds in his first four games of the season.

Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) and guard Luka Doncic defend Miami Heat forward Bobby Portis during a game Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

"Well, I think where he can continue to grow with us, you know, [with] per 36 numbers, per-minute numbers, he's not rebounding at the rate [we expect], and obviously that's a area of concern for us right now," Redick said. "So need to be him doing better, particularly on the defensive glass.

"… I remember my rookie year. B.J. Armstrong was an agent at Wasserman at the time and came down to Orlando to check on me. I remember when we were at the game, he was talking about Dwight [Howard]. He said, 'You know what Dwight's superpower is?' I said, 'No, I don't.' And he said, 'He can rebound outside his space. He can rebound outside his airspace.' And I've seen Walker do it. That's where I think he can give us the biggest boost right now defensively. We want him to protect the rim. We want him on the interior."

Redick wants Kessler to be everywhere. He's the heart of the Lakers' defense. He's wants one of the league's greatest rebounders and rim protectors to leap to the next level.

Following an 11-point, nine-rebound performance against the Clippers, he went on to have three straight double-doubles, averaging 13.6 points and 14.3 rebounds in a loss to the Warriors on Oct. 30 and wins over the Raptors on Nov. 1 and Heat on Nov. 2.

"I think his effort to go get the ball has been good," Redick said after the Lakers' 138-117 victory over the Heat on Monday. "I think he's boxed out really well."

Kessler is averaging 14.4 points, 10.2 rebounds and 2.7 blocked shots for the Lakers (7-2), who are in second place in the Western Conference.

Kessler is seventh in the NBA in rebounds. He's third in blocked shots.

They rank 21st in defensive rating (112.9) but first in offensive rating (121.1). Much of that is due to the team's steep drop-off when Kessler is off the court.

When Kessler is on the court? The Lakers' defensive rating is 110.1. When he's sidelined? It's 117.2, per Cleaning the Glass.

Kessler's impact on the Lakers has been notable.

His screening creates open shots for Dončić, who's averaging a league-leading 38.5 points, 8.1 rebounds and eight assists, and Austin Reaves, who's averaging 23.3 points on career highs in field-goal percentage (53.1) and 3-point percentage (42.9).

Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler dunks against the Sacramento Kings in an NBA basketball game in Sacramento, Calif., Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026. AP Photo/Scott Marshall

Kessler's presence in the paint has emboldened the team's perimeter defenders to be aggressive and take risks, which has led to the Lakers ranking No. 1 in opponent turnovers (166).

Kessler is ranked No. 1 among defenders who have taken at least 50 shots within 6 feet this season, holding opponents to 45.1% shooting.

But it's no secret that Kessler is still figuring things out with the Lakers.

The Lakers acquired Kessler in July in a sign-and-trade with Utah for two first-round picks and two first-round pick swaps on top of a four-year, $130 million deal.

Miami Heat center Bam Adebayo handles the ball as Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) defends on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

He's still working his way back from missing all but five games last season because of a torn posterior labrum in his left shoulder that required surgery.

Kessler is one of nine new players on the Lakers, and it's going to take time for him to learn Dončić and Reaves, in addition to developing into the defensive beast the team needs.

The Lakers need Kessler to be at his best to be a championship-level team.

He has steadily improved this season, though he stumbled a bit in Wednesday's 116-103 win over the Kings, finishing with 15 points, eight rebounds and a game-high four blocked shots. Kessler acknowledged that Domantas Sabonis (16 points, 17 rebounds) got the better of him, saying, "[He] definitely got my tail kicked a little bit in the first three quarters."

For Kessler, this is a learning process. There's going to be bumps in the road. Redick has preached patience.

And it's clear Redick wants him to reach new heights.
